   7 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Early childhood is a critical stage in children’s development since it sets the foundations for their socio-
emotional learning, cognitive development, health, nutrition, and later educational success (Costa et al., 
2019[1]; McCoy et al., 2017[2]; Richter et al., 2017[3]). Given this importance, enlarging children’s access 
to high-quality early childhood education and care (ECEC)—characterised by playful and meaningful 
interactions and developmentally appropriate environments that promote children’s development, well-
being, and learning (Needham and Ülküer, 2020[4]; OECD, 2020d[5])—is an important policy concern. 
Recognising the critical role of ECEC in children’s development, well-being, and learning, the OECD 
has developed a long-term data development strategy and a data collection roadmap to increase the 
knowledge base around ECEC systems (OECD, 2012b[6]; 2013[7]). To help countries make well-
informed policy choices, the OECD’s roadmap identified a significant need for new, higher-quality data 
on ECEC, including but not limited to: (a) staff-level data on aspects of process quality (OECD, 2018b[8]); 
(b) development, well-being and learning environments (namely, ECEC settings); and (c) child 
development, well-being, and learning outcomes (also referred to as child outcomes). The Starting 
Strong Teaching and Learning International Survey (TALIS Starting Strong) stems from this work and 
aims to monitor trends in the quality of ECEC settings. 
TALIS Starting Strong is a large-scale international survey of staff and leaders working in ECEC settings 
with children in pre-primary education, with an option to survey staff working with children under the age 
of three. To monitor trends, TALIS Starting Strong collects long-term data about workforce 
characteristics, working conditions, and the nature of the work performed in ECEC settings across 
different countries. As a survey of the ECEC workforce, the focus of TALIS Starting Strong is to provide 
information on factors that have been shown to influence children’s development, well-being, and 
learning. 
Quality ECEC settings are conceptualised in the international literature as having two main dimensions: 
process quality and structural quality. Process quality pertains to children’s daily interactions in their 
ECEC settings (including with ECEC staff, with other children in their classroom or playgroup, with 
families, and with the wider community), as well as the features of the learning environment and the 
materials used in everyday ECEC provision (OECD, 2021[9]). In other words, process quality refers to 
the factors most proximal to the child’s experiences in ECEC; it is the mechanism responsible for child 
development, well-being and learning outcomes (Pianta et al., 2009[10]). Structural quality refers to 
factors that are less proximal to child development, yet are easier to measure and regulate; such as 
staff-child ratios, class/playgroup size, and staff qualifications and training. Factors related to structural 
quality are considered to be important preconditions of process quality (Slot et al., 2015[11]). Both 
process quality and structural quality were captured in TALIS Starting Strong 2018, and they continue 
to be measured in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
The survey is intended as an internationally comparable survey of ECEC programme leaders and staff. 
It examines aspects related to the quality of development, well-being, and learning environments in 
ECEC settings by asking questions on staff beliefs and the types of pedagogical practices they deploy. 
Hence, the survey provides a suitable and economically feasible alternative or complement to 
systematic observation measures of process quality. After all, while such observation measures can 
1 Introduction 

8    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
achieve reliability and validity, they remain technically and financially challenging for many countries to 
collect and use. 
In addition, the survey captures the perspective of ECEC staff, which is often overlooked in available 
process quality instruments. TALIS Starting Strong captures valuable information on ECEC staff 
relationships with other staff, leaders, parents, and the community (OECD, 2018b[8]). The survey also 
seeks to inform policy around motivating, developing, and retaining staff in the profession. It therefore 
asks about ECEC job satisfaction, ECEC setting climate, ECEC leadership, and information related to 
ECEC staff turnover. The survey also gathers information on ECEC staff’s educational background, as 
well as barriers and facilitators to continuing professional development. 
 The first cycle of TALIS Starting Strong in 2018 produced many policy-relevant findings, and it 
demonstrated the feasibility of an internationally comparable study of ECEC. It provided insights into 
individual indicators, as well as relationships between those indicators, both between and within 
countries. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2018 involved nine countries that collected data from staff working with children 
in pre-primary education1 (ISCED 02)2. In addition, four of these countries administered the survey for 
staff working with children under the age of three3. Each of these modules included a staff and leader 
questionnaire. Given the diversity of ECEC services and structures, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 also 
included a combined questionnaire version of the survey for very small settings (i.e., home-based 
settings, or settings with only one staff member). An expanded version of the combined questionnaire 
was also developed. This expanded version incorporated additional questions to account for the fact 
that leaders are also teachers in Israel’s ISCED 02 provision. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is the second cycle of the survey and will involve a larger number of 
participating countries. The survey builds on the information collected in the first cycle to facilitate trend 
comparisons. It also continues to collect data from both pre-primary staff and staff working with children 
under the age of three using three types of questionnaires (staff questionnaire, leader questionnaire, 
and combined questionnaire). There are two versions of the combined questionnaire: (i) one aimed at 
very small centres with only one staff member or at home-based settings; and (ii) another aimed at 
centres whose leaders also perform staff duties. Both versions of the combined questionnaire take 
questions from both the staff questionnaire and the leader questionnaire. 
It is important to note that this second cycle refines rather than redevelops the survey. While the TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024 Conceptual Framework is largely anchored on the conceptual framework of TALIS 
Starting Strong 2018, the former also includes updates to the latter. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
incorporates new, emerging evidence to strengthen the theoretical underpinnings of the survey. This 
new evidence also ensures the survey’s relevance across the larger, more diverse group of countries 
participating in the second cycle. 
Continuing from the first cycle, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 still gathers information from ECEC staff and 
leaders on factors that contribute to children’s development, well-being, and learning, including: (a) 
pedagogical practices used by ECEC staff; (b) characteristics of ECEC staff and settings; (c) working 
conditions and job satisfaction; and (d) features of high-quality development, well-being, and learning 
environments (e.g., pedagogical leadership, safety of the ECEC setting, staff-child ratio and 
interactions). 
While TALIS Starting Strong 2024 retains many content areas covered by TALIS Starting Strong 2018, 
this second cycle nonetheless presents an opportunity to refine ways to capture information on the 
survey’s content areas and constructs of interest. Furthermore, it provides an avenue to collect data 
regarding issues of increasing relevance and of growing priority to the ECEC field since TALIS Starting 
Strong was last administered in 2018. 

   9 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
This document lays out the conceptual framework for TALIS Starting Strong 2024 and is organised into 
four sections, as follows: 
• 
Section I discusses the purpose and goals of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 – namely, to inform 
policies (principles, rules, and guidelines) that could be adopted by governments and education 
systems to support their long-term goals and strategies regarding ECEC. This implies a focus 
on factors that are amenable and malleable to change at the ECEC system, setting, and staff 
levels. Section I also provides an overview of the survey’s links to TALIS, as well as key insights 
obtained through the first cycle of TALIS Starting Strong. 
• 
Section II provides an overview of how the focus of TALIS Starting Strong was established. It 
also lays out content areas that have been retained from the 2018 cycle, as well as new areas 
that are explored in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. It also updates the ‘TALIS Starting Strong 
Conceptual Mapping of ECEC Environment for Children’s Development, Well-Being and 
Learning’ (Sim et al., 2019[12]). In doing so, it highlights the central role of ECEC staff in 
mediating children's experiences of ECEC provision and reflects new and emerging content 
areas for the 2024 cycle. 
• 
Section III elaborates on the survey’s underpinning content areas by laying out their theoretical 
foundation and discussing empirical findings of previous relevant research. The section 
proceeds along four distinct but interrelated themes: 
o 
Staff practices with children (including: aspects of process quality; and the monitoring 
and assessment of children’s development, well-being and learning). 
o 
ECEC staff and leader characteristics (including: staff and leader demographic and 
education backgrounds; professional development; well-being; professional beliefs 
about children’s development, well-being and learning; and self-efficacy). 
o 
ECEC setting characteristics (including: structural quality characteristics; pedagogical 
and administrative leadership; organisational climate and working conditions; and 
stakeholder relations). 
o 
Cross-cutting content areas (diversity, equity, and inclusion in ECEC settings; 
approaches to comprehensive and continuous child supports; fostering competent 
ECEC settings; and the use of digital technologies to support staff and leaders). 
• 
Section IV discusses the survey design and methodology for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. It 
covers the sampling approach, definition of the target population, and actual survey procedures 
(including initial development of the survey instrument, pilot, field trial, and main survey 
administration).

12    
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
TALIS Starting Strong is a large-scale international survey of staff and leaders in early childhood 
education and care (ECEC) settings. It is complemented by other activities and studies in the larger 
OECD programme of work. 
Overview of TALIS Starting Strong 20241 
Objective and purpose as an indicator study 
TALIS Starting Strong aims to provide robust international indicators and policy-relevant analysis on 
ECEC staff and leaders, pedagogical and professional practices in ECEC settings, and the 
development, well-being, and learning environments frequented by children in ECEC settings. 
Moreover, it describes how the aforesaid aspects of ECEC provision vary within and between countries, 
and how they change (or do not change) over time. In collecting cross-country data, TALIS Starting 
Strong acknowledges that indicators will inevitably be influenced by differences in cultural norms and 
values. 
The indicators and analyses produced by TALIS Starting Strong intend to help countries and jurisdictions 
review and develop policies that facilitate child development, well-being, and learning. Given TALIS 
Starting Strong’s focus on policy relevance, the survey covers indicators that have been theoretically 
and empirically found to be associated with children’s positive development and learning outcomes. 
The guiding principles of TALIS Starting Strong are aligned with those from the core TALIS at ISCED 
level 2. These are as follows: 
• 
Policy relevance: Clarity about key policy issues and a focus on the questions that are 
most relevant for participating countries are essential. 
• 
Value added: International comparisons should be a significant source of the study’s 
benefits. 
• 
Indicator-oriented: The results should yield information that can be used to develop 
indicators. 
• 
Validity, reliability, comparability, and rigour: Based on a rigorous review of the 
knowledge base, the survey should yield information that is valid, reliable, and comparable 
across participating countries. 
• 
Interpretability: Participating countries should be able to interpret the results in a 
meaningful way. 
2 The role and policy relevance of 
TALIS Starting Strong as an 
evolving study 

   13 
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
• 
Efficiency and cost-effectiveness: The work should be carried out in a timely and cost-
effective way. 
 
Like the first cycle, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 will produce: 
• 
Indicators that monitor ECEC systems at the levels of staff and settings (including those 
related to leaders, as well as staff who work directly with children). 
• 
A reliable, comparative database providing within- and between-country data on: (a) 
characteristics of ECEC staff and ECEC setting leaders; (b) their pedagogical practices with 
children; (c) professional practices in other aspects of their work; and (d) development, well-
being, and learning environments. 
 
Analyses of the survey indicators aim to elucidate the context and associations across: (a) ECEC staff 
and leaders’ characteristics; (b) ECEC staff and leaders’ pedagogical and professional practices; and 
(c) the different features of development, well-being, and learning environments. Furthermore, the 
results of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 will be a source of information for the OECD’s education indicators 
programme, which seeks to prompt policy discourse, help shape international public policy, and inform 
decision-making within the ECEC and larger education system of participating countries. 
Countries participating in TALIS Starting Strong have made it a priority to ensure that at least some 
indicators can be compared across cycles and education levels covered by the TALIS programme (see 
Section I). For TALIS Starting Strong 2024, this need for comparison means retaining certain questions 
from TALIS Starting Strong 2018 to start conducting trend analyses. However, the need for comparison 
does not preclude making changes that would improve on how concepts are measured in the survey. It 
also does not preclude introducing new items that would cover areas deemed relevant for the 2024 
cycle. 
Main areas for policy considerations 
The policy issues identified for the first cycle remain relevant for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. The 
development of the first cycle’s conceptual framework was guided by the document titled “Towards a 
Conceptual Framework for an International Survey on ECEC Staff”, which was prepared in the early 
phases of developing TALIS Starting Strong (Loizillon, 2016[1]). This document was based on 
discussions with the OECD ECEC Network, which brought together: (a) international policymakers and 
researchers; (b) international organisations, such as the European Commission and UNESCO; (c) a 
subgroup of this Network, comprised of countries that were initially interested and eventually participated 
in TALIS Starting Strong; and (d) other external experts. 
The TALIS Starting Strong 2024 Conceptual Framework is the result of an iterative process: concepts 
formulated by the ECEC Questionnaire Expert Group (ECEC QEG) were revised following discussions 
with relevant stakeholders. The ECEC QEG includes subject experts in the field of early childhood and 
experts in survey methodology. The ECEC QEG works in close collaboration with members of the 
international research consortium, the Chair of the TALIS QEG, the OECD Secretariat, and the 
Technical Advisory Group (TAG). 
 In developing the Conceptual Framework, the ECEC QEG considered: (a) participating countries’ 
priority areas; (b) theoretical literature; (c) key developments and debates on the subject; and (d) the 
indicators’ analytical potential. Discussions and consultations after the first cycle of data collection 
helped identify ways to refine and focus the policy considerations for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. For 
instance, the importance of diversity, equity, and inclusion was a salient point in these discussions; this 
has led to diversity, equity, and inclusion becoming a core policy issue for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 

14    
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
In addition, the first cycle’s policy issues have been streamlined from three to two for TALIS Starting 
Strong 2024, owing to the overlap in the scope of the second and third policy issues. As such, building 
on the foundational work from the first cycle, the main policy issues for the second cycle are: 
• 
Ensuring quality and equity in learning and well-being for all children. This includes 
the following questions: what pedagogical practices are used by ECEC staff? How do staff 
support children’s development, well-being, and learning? How do staff and leaders adapt 
practices to children’s individual needs and different family backgrounds? Do children from 
different family backgrounds experience a similar quality of development, well-being, and 
learning environments? Are staff prepared to address the needs of an increasingly diverse 
child population? What are staff beliefs around issues relating to equity and diversity in 
ECEC settings? 
• 
Motivating, developing, and retaining staff in the profession. This includes the following 
questions: how are ECEC staff trained? What are the barriers and facilitators of staff 
professional development? How satisfied are ECEC staff with their profession? How do 
ECEC staff describe their well-being and sources of stress? How much turnover is there 
among ECEC staff? 
 
Staff- and setting-level contextual information that cuts across these different policy issues was also 
deemed necessary to properly interpret the results and to better address questions like: 
• 
Who are the ECEC staff in participating countries/sub-national territories? How do their 
personal characteristics (e.g., demographic background, initial training and professional 
development, employment status, level of experience) compare? 
• 
What settings do they work in? How do these settings and working environments compare? 
Links to TALIS developed for other levels of education 
TALIS Starting Strong, as part of the TALIS programme, focuses on staff and leaders working in ECEC. 
The core survey of TALIS focuses on teachers and principals working in lower secondary education 
(ISCED level 2). TALIS Starting Strong 2024 was developed in close co-ordination with the team 
developing the core TALIS questionnaires and has been firmly integrated into the TALIS programme for 
the 2024 cycle. This integration is described in the text that follows. 
The development of TALIS Starting Strong was based on the same principles guiding the core TALIS 
survey. As discussed in Section I, the development process sought to maintain comparability between 
cycles to start building trend analyses, refine and improve the quality of questions, and maximise 
alignments and synergies with the TALIS programme for other levels of education, particularly for ISCED 
level 2 and primary education (ISCED level 1). Content areas were aligned across surveys, while 
acknowledging that item-level differences would be necessary to ensure the applicability of TALIS 
Starting Strong to the ECEC context. 
A guiding concept paper set the direction for TALIS 2024, including for TALIS Starting Strong. This first 
step aimed to establish thematic alignment across surveys. Through a literature review, as well as 
consultations with participating countries and jurisdictions, four areas of growing priority were identified, 
as follows: 
1. Diversity, equity, and inclusion in ECEC settings; 
2. Approaches to comprehensive/continuous child supports; 
3. Fostering competent ECEC settings; and 
4. Digital technologies to support leaders and staff. 

   15 
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 and TALIS 2024 share some common areas of growing relevance: 
Diversity and Equity, Socio-Emotional Learning, and Educational Use of Technology. Issues related to 
diversity and equity are highly relevant across educational levels. Ensuring children’s holistic 
development, which includes their social and emotional development, is a long-standing focus of ECEC; 
hence, it remains relevant for the second cycle (Schäfer, 2019[2]). In addition, there is growing interest 
in supporting students’ social and emotional development at later educational stages, acknowledging 
how older learners could also benefit from the integrated education and care approach used in ECEC. 
There is also increased interest in fostering competent ECEC systems that can respond rapidly and 
effectively to changing circumstances at a local, national, or international level. Finally, there is an 
emerging need to improve our understanding of the role of digital technology in ECEC provision and 
how it can be used for various aspects of ECEC staff’s work. Common policy issues addressed by both 
TALIS 2024 and TALIS Starting Strong 2024 are outlined in Table 2.1 below. 
Further alignment is made at the level of survey governance and implementation. For example, countries 
are encouraged to have one national survey centre for both projects. In addition, a number of experts 
sit in the respective Questionnaire Expert Groups (QEGs) of both TALIS 2024 and TALIS Starting Strong 
2024. The two studies also share one Technical Advisory Group (TAG).

settings for children under age 3) 
TALIS 2024 (ISCED 1, 2 and 3) 
1. Ensuring quality and equity in development, 
well-being, and learning environments for all

1. Quality teachers and teaching 
5. School policies and effectiveness 
2. Motivating, developing and retaining staff in 
the profession 
2. Attracting teachers to the profession 
4. Retaining teachers in the profession 
3. Developing teachers within the profession 
Insights from TALIS Starting Strong 2018 
Findings from TALIS Starting Strong 2018 are reported in three main OECD publications: (i) ‘Providing 
Quality Early Childhood Education and Care’ (OECD, 2019c[3]); (ii) ‘Building a High-Quality Early 
Childhood Education and Care Workforce’ (OECD, 2020b[4]); and ‘Quality Early Childhood Education 
and Care for Children Under Age 3’ (OECD, 2020f[5]). Key findings are summarised below. 
Regarding results related to staff beliefs about skills and abilities that will prepare children for life in the 
future, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 found that ECEC staff considered helping children develop the 
capacity to cooperate with others to be their highest priority. This priority is followed by supporting the 
development of children’s oral language and enquiry skills, then the ability to think creatively. 
Regarding staff initial preparation and continuous professional development, TALIS Starting Strong 
2018 found that a majority of staff in participating countries had received pre-service training with a focus 
on working with children. In addition, it reported that a majority of staff in all countries had participated 
in continuing professional development activities in the 12 months preceding the survey. 
In addition, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 observed that the breadth and depth of in-service training 
matters. In all countries, staff who received pre-service and in-service training that covered a wider 
range of content areas reported adapting their practices to children’s needs – that is, compared to staff 
whose training covered less content. Survey findings also showed how more training in a given area is 
associated with an increase in staff’s sense of self-efficacy to support children’s development. Results 

16    
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
also indicated that, in ECEC settings that serve more diverse groups of children, there is a larger 
proportion of staff who received training on how to work with children from diverse backgrounds. 
Broadly speaking, ECEC staff surveyed for TALIS Starting Strong 2018 reported having high levels of 
job satisfaction. However, both ECEC staff and leaders expressed mixed views regarding their working 
conditions, with many indicating a low level of satisfaction with their salaries. It was also found that staff 
working in more challenging settings were not systematically afforded better working conditions. 
ECEC staff reported the following as being their main sources of stress: (a) lack of resources; (b) having 
too many children in the classroom or playroom; (c) having too much work related to documenting 
children’s development; and (d) having too much administrative work. In addition, staff across all 
countries reported feeling more valued by the children they serve and their parents or guardians than 
by society. Staff across all participating countries also agreed that reducing group size, improving staff 
salaries, and receiving support to help them serve children with special needs should be important 
spending priorities. However, the results of TALIS Starting Strong 2018 indicated that monitoring 
activities were more likely to focus on assessing facilities and setting finances than on the quality of 
staff-child interactions. 
The reports also explored leadership and management in ECEC settings. They found pedagogical 
leadership to be positively associated with staff attitudes and practices, which were in turn linked to 
quality of ECEC provision. Furthermore, staff who perceived their leaders as employing a more 
distributed form of leadership tended to engage more frequently in collaborative practices and reported 
higher levels of job satisfaction. 
In all participating countries, ECEC leaders reported that they often communicated with parents or 
guardians through both formal and informal channels While staff communication with parents was found 
to be common, fewer staff reported encouraging parents to play and conduct learning activities with their 
children at home. ECEC setting collocation with primary schools was found, in several countries, to be 
associated with greater frequency of communication with primary school staff. Collocated ECEC settings 
also tended to more frequently conduct activities targeted at parents or guardians to facilitate children’s 
transitions across educational levels. 
Given these findings, staff-child interactions, initial training and continuous professional development of 
ECEC staff and leaders, working conditions, leadership, and stakeholder relations in ECEC settings 
continue to be areas of interest for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. All of these themes are relevant to the 
areas of growing priority identified for the second cycle (see Section 2). 
The second cycle of TALIS Starting Strong presents an opportunity to refine the data obtained through 
the survey instrument. The 2018 cycle of TALIS Starting Strong revealed that the methods used to 
capture information on the quality of staff-child interactions (or process quality) could still be improved 
to facilitate data analysis and interpretation. Hence, developing TALIS Starting Strong 2024 involved 
exploring alternative ways to capture this information. 
Moreover, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 asked staff to think about a specific ‘target group’ to encourage 
respondents to think concretely when answering certain questions about their work with children. While 
the concept of a ‘target group’ is difficult to align across countries and settings, it is a useful way to 
prompt ECEC staff to think about their practices in a specific context. Thus, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
retains this notion of a ‘target group’, although the definition was slightly revised in an effort to make the 
construct more relevant across diverse ECEC systems.  
Diversity, equity, and inclusion in ECEC settings was identified as an area of growing priority for TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024. Therefore, it was important for the questionnaires to capture the degree of diversity 
of children attending ECEC settings across all participating countries and jurisdictions. Strategies to 
improve capturing information on this included revisiting the response options and definitions on the 

   17 
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
percentage of children from different backgrounds – for instance, ‘socio-economically disadvantaged 
homes’ or ‘refugees’ – to increase the granularity of the data collected. 
Staff diversity was also important to capture. This includes the amount of ethnic and linguistic diversity 
of staff in ECEC settings, and the extent to which the background of staff matches that of the child group. 
Staff training on adopting inclusive practices to ensure that all children can participate fully in their 
learning experiences is another core dimension of the ‘diversity, equity, and inclusion’ content area. 
Other areas of growing priority that are covered by TALIS Starting Strong 2024 include but are not 
limited to: (a) ECEC staff’s preference for part-time work; (b) reasons for joining the profession; (c) 
perceived usefulness of continuous professional development; (d) staff sense of self-efficacy; (e) staff 
development needs regarding the use of digital technologies; and (f) resources available to ECEC 
settings.

To define the focus of TALIS Starting Strong 2024, consultations with the OECD ECEC Network and 
the ECEC QEG were undertaken following a targeted literature review that was used to inform the scope 
of TALIS Starting Strong 2024. The literature review and subsequent consultations concluded that the 
policy issues and content areas defined for TALIS Starting Strong 2018 remain broadly valid for TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024. In addition, four areas of growing priority, which had been discussed in Section I, 
were identified for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
Prioritisation of content areas for TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
In the first cycle, a priority rating exercise and a consultation process helped to identify content areas 
that would inform the main policy issues. The TALIS Starting Strong 2018 Conceptual Framework (Sim 
et al., 2019[1]) presents a detailed account of this process. The second cycle continues to explore the 
12 content areas addressed by TALIS Starting Strong 2018, albeit with updated labelling. The labelling 
was updated for precision and conciseness, as well as to better reflect the focus of TALIS Starting Strong 
2024. Table 3.1 presents the themes addressed in 2024 and their corresponding labels from 2018.

3 The focus of TALIS Starting Strong 
2024 

   19 
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
To bolster the relevance of TALIS Starting Strong 2024, the TALIS 2024 International Consortium1 
conducted a targeted literature review that included evidence published after 2018. This literature review 
sought to update the survey’s theoretical underpinnings using the most current evidence. In addition, 
this review identified trends and new developments that could guide the direction of subsequent TALIS 
Starting Strong cycles. 
The identified topics correspond with the content areas established for TALIS Starting Strong 2018. As 
mentioned in Section I, these areas include issues related: (a) diversity, equity, and inclusion in ECEC 
settings; (b) approaches to comprehensive/continuous child supports; (c) ways of fostering competent 
ECEC settings; and (d) the role of digital technologies in supporting leaders and staff. However, while 
the content areas of the 2018 and 2024 cycle align, the 2024 cycle iterates on the 2018 cycle by shifting 
the focus and/or formulation of existing survey items, as well as by introducing new questions or items 
while minimising respondent burden as much as possible. These new cross-cutting content areas are 
described in detail in Section III. 
In January 2021, the initial results from the targeted literature review were presented and discussed 
during separate consultations with the ECEC QEG and the OECD ECEC Network. Feedback from these 
consultations was used to refine how priority areas are framed in the survey. For instance, countries 
voiced interest in exploring the use of digital technology in ECEC. In doing so, they stressed their interest 
in understanding how digital technology is used by staff to facilitate their own learning and practice, 
rather than how staff use digital tools in their interactions with children. 
Following the iterations and refinements described above, the areas of growing priority were discussed 
and approved at the 27th OECD ECEC Network meeting. The previously described consultations 
established how the policy issues and content areas covered by TALIS Starting Strong 2018 remain 
relevant for the 2024 cycle. It was also agreed that the areas of growing priority identified through this 
consultation process should be explored in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
Participating countries and economies, as well as the OECD ECEC Network, were asked to rank the 
identified areas of growing priority in order of importance, through a priority rating exercise conducted 
in May 2021. They were also asked to identify questions from TALIS Starting Strong 2018 that should 
be retained to enable trend analyses in the second cycle. This prioritisation exercise was open to all 
members of the ECEC Network, as well as to all confirmed participants in the second cycle. Participation 
in this exercise was voluntary. 
It should be noted that there was significant variation in the way in which countries ranked the areas of 
growing priority. The resulting prioritisation aligned more closely with the priorities of some countries 
over others. Moreover, not all countries implementing TALIS Starting Strong 2024 participated in this 
prioritisation exercise. 
Seventeen stakeholders (including countries, jurisdictions, and a civil society organisation)2 provided 
input on the areas of growing priority. While views differed, diversity, equity, and inclusion was judged 
to be the most important. This was followed by approaches to comprehensive and continuous child 
supports, then by fostering competent ECEC systems, and finally, by the use of digital technologies to 
support ECEC staff and leaders. 
Eighteen stakeholders3 rated the staff and leader survey questions, indicating: (a) whether a question 
was of high priority and (b) if a question should be kept the same, kept with modifications, kept but 
considered low-priority, or dropped. The group decided that over 70% and 80% of the questions in the 
staff and leader questionnaire, respectively, should be kept but with modifications. Around 15% of the 
questions from both the staff and leader questionnaires were considered high-priority. Very few 
questions from both the staff and leader questionnaires were judged appropriate to drop or to be of low 
priority. 

20    
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
These results indicate participating countries and jurisdictions’ continued interest in exploring the same 
content areas as the 2018 cycle while also refining survey questions, in the interest of improving data 
quality and ensuring that the identified areas of growing priority are covered. 
Differences within ECEC 
The above issues are broadly relevant across the two populations of interest for TALIS Starting Strong: 
ECEC staff and leaders working with children under the age of three, and those working in pre-primary 
education. TALIS Starting Strong explores the same policy questions and content areas at both levels. 
However, there may be differences between settings serving either population, especially in countries 
or jurisdictions where ECEC for the said age groups is delivered separately. These differences were 
considered throughout survey development. For instance, the initial preparation and professional 
development received by ECEC staff serving either age group, as well as the practices they use; staff 
serving younger children might place a stronger emphasis on the care dimension of ECEC. In addition, 
organisational structures may also differ between ECEC settings serving either age group, hence 
requiring different approaches to leadership or stakeholder relations. 
The complexities of ECEC systems are highlighted by the classification of ECEC settings in the 
International Standard Classification of Education (ISCED). ISCED is an instrument for compiling 
international education statistics, with ECEC classified as ISCED level 0. Within ISCED level 0, 
programmes are often further differentiated by age: early childhood educational development serving 
children under the age of three is categorised as ISCED level 01, and pre-primary education serving 
children from age three to primary school age is categorised as ISCED level 02. Pre-primary settings in 
TALIS Starting Strong meet the definition for ISCED level 02, whereas settings serving children under 
the age of three are not required to meet the formal definition for ISCED level 01. 
These variations were considered when designing TALIS Starting Strong 2018, and they remain 
important for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 continues to use tailored 
response options to account for variations between staff working with children under the age of three, 
compared to those working with older pre-primary children. 
Generating evidence specific to ECEC settings serving children under the age of three continues to be 
relevant, especially since this age group remains understudied (Cadima et al., 2020[2]). However, while 
pertinent evidence remains limited, the available literature indicates that, despite a sharp increase in 
access to ECEC in OECD countries, enrolment for children under the age of three remains low in many 
countries (OECD, 2018a[3]; Schleicher, 2019[4]). 
As such, there is a need to obtain more fine-grained information about aspects of process quality – 
specifically for younger children – as well as information about the beliefs, practices, professional 
supports, and experiences of ECEC staff working with younger children. Some of these aspects are 
suitable for measurement in TALIS Starting Strong (Cadima et al., 2020[2]). This information can also 
provide valuable insight into the extent to which these processes may vary across younger children of 
diverse backgrounds. Thus, TALIS Starting Strong can generate insight regarding equity in ECEC 
provision – between and within countries – for children under the age of three. 
It is also important to note that ECEC is delivered across diverse institutional configurations. ECEC 
settings cover a wide range of arrangements, ranging from centre-based (i.e., located within rooms 
specifically dedicated to ECEC, either in a standalone building or within a school) to home-based (i.e., 
within rooms in homes of the corresponding ECEC staff; OECD (2019e[5])). Settings also vary in the size 
of their staffing complement and in the way roles and responsibilities are distributed across ECEC setting 
staff. For instance, some countries have ECEC settings staffed by only one person caring for a small 

   21 
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
number of children, as well as settings where ECEC staff members simultaneously perform leadership 
responsibilities and work directly with children. 
To account for these different institutional configurations, the second cycle includes combined 
questionnaires which incorporate questions from both the staff and leader questionnaires. The 
combined questionnaire appears in two versions: one version is aimed at (i) very small centres with only 
one staff member or home-based settings while the other is aimed at (ii) centres whose leaders also 
perform staff duties. 
Overview of conceptual mapping of content areas and their relationships 
A conceptual model was developed as part of the first cycle of TALIS Starting Strong to describe the 
ECEC system, staff, and leader characteristics, as well as the quality of ECEC development, well-being, 
and learning environments (Sim et al., 2019[1]). This model laid out the survey’s theoretical 
underpinnings and is described in detail in the TALIS Starting Strong 2018 Conceptual Framework. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 continues to be grounded on the 2018 conceptual model but updates it to 
reflect the centrality of staff and leaders as the survey respondents. It also continues to capture various 
aspects of ECEC provision that are considered relevant and influential to children's development, well-
being, and learning. 
Drawing on Bronfenbrenner's (1979[6]) ecological systems framework, the updated conceptual mapping 
for TALIS Starting Strong 2024 seeks to showcase that children’s development, well-being and learning, 
while at the centre of ECEC provision, are interrelated and influenced by the wider environment in which 
children operate. The updated mapping also reflects international conceptualisations of quality ECEC, 
such as the structure-process model of quality (Kluczniok and Roßbach, 2014[7]); which conceives 
quality ECEC as being made up of four interrelated components: structural characteristics, educational 
beliefs, educational processes, and networking with families. The conceptual mapping for TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024 is illustrated in Figure 3.1. To account for the structural and organisational 
heterogeneity of ECEC provision in participating countries and jurisdictions, the TALIS Starting Strong 
2024 conceptual mapping, like the 2018 conceptual model, is conceived broadly and comprehensively 
– reflecting all main spheres of the ECEC ecosystem. 

22    
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Figure 3.1. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 conceptual mapping of ECEC environment for children’s 
development, well-being, and learning (D, WB & L) 
 
 
Figure 3.1 above shows that children are situated in the classroom or playroom, where they interact with 
staff and peers; the nature of those interactions are influenced by the characteristics, knowledge, and 
beliefs of staff and their pedagogical approach. These staff-child and child-child interactions are situated 
within the larger ECEC setting, where leaders influence the institutional setting, including goals, 
resources, management practices, and working conditions. Staff and leaders interact with each other, 
and, as such, leaders can influence staff practices and child experiences in the classroom or playroom 
and setting through leadership approaches and decisions. In turn, ECEC settings are themselves 
located in a broader community, where other stakeholders – such as children’s families, other 
educational institutions, and other service providers – may interact with children, setting leaders, and 
staff. Such stakeholders can range from the families of the participating children, whose role is central 
in providing safe and nurturing interactions during infancy and early childhood which will positively affect 
development and well-being, to other educational institutions, and other service providers. 

   23 
 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Subsequently, the community environment and settings are influenced by the wider ECEC policy at 
local, regional and national levels. 
The survey supports the collection of information at the classroom or playroom level and at the ECEC 
setting level, as reported by ECEC staff and leaders. While children are at the centre of this model – 
given how ECEC systems’ ultimate objective is to support children and their families – the experiences 
and practices of ECEC setting staff and leaders are equally important. 
The survey’s content areas are organised into four dimensions: (1) staff practices with children 
(specifically elements related to process quality); (2) ECEC setting characteristics; (3) staff and leader 
characteristics; and (4) cross-cutting content areas that are linked to the three other dimensions and 
that apply across the various levels of the ECEC ecosystem. The conceptual mapping in Figure 3.1 
illustrates where the four dimensions are situated within the ECEC ecosystem. 
Staff practices with children are captured by indicators pertaining to: (a) process quality; (b) approaches 
to monitoring and assessment; (c) professional beliefs about children’s development, well-being, and 
learning; and (d) self-efficacy. The second dimension, ECEC setting characteristics, is constituted by 
indicators relating to: (a) the structural quality of the environment; (b) pedagogical and administrative 
leadership of the ECEC setting; (c) organisational climate and working conditions; and (d) stakeholder 
relations. Staff and leader characteristics represent the third dimension, which pertain to staff and 
leaders’: (a) demographic background; (b) formal, initial preparation for working with young children; (c) 
continuing professional development; and (d) well-being. Finally, as discussed in Section I, the cross-
cutting content areas concern: (a) diversity, equity and inclusion in ECEC settings; (b) approaches to 
comprehensive and continuous supports for children; (c) competent ECEC systems; and (d) use of 
digital technology to support ECEC staff and leaders. 
It is assumed that the factors subsumed within the four dimensions influence each other. However, 
Figure 3.1 does not show paths of influence, partly because the conceptual mapping intends to 
represent broad content areas rather than defined indicators, and partly to account for the large number 
of potential interrelationships across content areas. In addition, the relationships between content areas 
(or their corresponding indicators) could be unidirectional or bidirectional. 
Finally, while TALIS Starting Strong 2024 can provide valuable information about associations between 
dimensions and indicators, the survey data does not seek to make causal inferences about those 
associations. The main goal of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is thus for each factor across the different 
dimensions to contribute to the knowledge base regarding the main policy issues guiding the survey, 
namely: (i) ensuring quality and equity in development, well-being, and learning environments for all 
children (Policy Issue 1); and (ii) motivating, developing, and retaining staff in the profession (Policy 
Issue 2). These factors are discussed in more detail in the respective thematic sub-sections in Section 
III. These sub-sections also outline how the content areas interrelate with each other.
