TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
This section provides an overview of research related to each of the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 content 
areas. The description of each content area is accompanied by a definition, a short review of related 
literature, a list of relevant indicators based on research, and a discussion of the extent to which the 
content area can be suitably explored by TALIS Starting Strong. 
Staff practices with children 
The Staff Practices with Children dimension is comprised of four content areas that closely relate to the 
ways staff deliver pedagogy and support to children; these undergird the quality of ECEC provision. 
These four content areas are: (i) process quality that focuses on staff-child interactions; (ii) monitoring 
and assessment of children’s development, well-being, and learning; (iii) beliefs about children’s 
development, well-being, and learning; and (iv) self-efficacy. 
Process quality 
As discussed in the previous section, early childhood experts generally conceptualise the quality of 
ECEC settings as having two main dimensions: process quality and structural quality. This sub-section 
focuses on process quality; structural quality is discussed in a later sub-section. 
In the ECEC context, process quality usually refers to children’s daily experiences in the ECEC setting 
that may foster their development, well-being, and learning. These include the nature of children’s 
interactions with adults and with other children, with the physical environment and learning materials, 
with their families, and with the larger community [see, for example: Burchinal (2018[1]), Hamre (2014[2]), 
Layzer and Goodson (2006[3]), Mashburn et al. (2008[4]), OECD (2021[5]), Pianta et al (2005[6])]. In the 
ECEC setting, these processes may refer to positive and nurturing relationships with staff, classroom 
routines or pedagogical practices employed by staff such as scaffolding, posing questions and 
modelling. 
For the purposes of TALIS Starting Strong, process quality is primarily focused on the quality of staff-
child interactions and, more specifically, on the pedagogical practices of ECEC staff. This 
conceptualisation aligns with how process quality is most commonly operationalised in the literature 
(McNally and Slutsky, 2020[7]; Pianta and Hofkens, 2023[8]; Hofkens, Pianta and Hamre, 2023[9]; OECD, 
2018b[10]). However, even with this more specific conceptualisation, pedagogical practices comprise a 
wide range of activities and approaches, including but not limited to domain-specific instructional 
practices, learning through play, classroom or playroom management, and emotional support provided 
to children within the classroom or playroom environment. Hence, excellent process quality is 
conceptualised in TALIS Starting Strong as a repertoire of practices, rather than being typified by any 
one pedagogical practice. 
4 A closer look at content areas for 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 

26    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
TALIS Starting Strong also recognises the influence of the quality of interactions between different 
stakeholders in the ECEC setting – for example, interactions between children, between ECEC staff, 
between children and ECEC staff, between children and parents, and between staff and parents – on 
children’s development, well-being, and learning. As such, the survey also aims to capture select 
information on the nature of these inter-stakeholder interactions. Staff-parent interactions are particularly 
addressed in the Stakeholder Relations content area. 
In research that aims to measure process quality, trained assessors typically observe staff-child 
interactions in their natural classroom or playroom settings and rate staff practices using well-validated 
tools and rubrics [for example: the Environment Rating Scales developed by Harms et al. (2014[11]), 
Harms et al. (2017[12]), and the Classroom Assessment Scoring System, or CLASS, developed by Pianta 
et al. (2008[13])]. Other observation-based tools have been developed to measure the nature of child-
child interactions as well. The development of observational assessment tools remains an active area 
of research, with research efforts aiming to address persistent issues of validity and inter-rater reliability 
and to develop tools that can be used across diverse settings, within and across countries (Pianta, 
Hamre and Nguyen, 2020[14]; Raikes, Davis and Burton, 2019[15]; Sokolovic et al., 2021[16]; UNESCO 
et al., 2017[17]). 
The proceeding text appraises the literature that has shaped understandings of how process quality 
influences children’s development, well-being, and learning. Afterwards, it discusses options for 
capturing data regarding process quality in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Cognition, including higher-order cognitive processes like self-regulation, undergoes markedly rapid 
development during early childhood (Montroy et al., 2016[18]; Asmussen et al., 2018[19]). At the same 
time, young children’s growing regulatory capacity and motivational systems are inextricably linked to 
their socio-emotional development (National Scientific Council on the Developing Child, 2015[20]). This 
early socio-emotional development is defined by the Collaborative for Academic, Social and Emotional 
Learning (CASEL) as being constituted by five core social and emotional competencies: self-awareness, 
self-management, responsible decision-making, relationship skills, and social awareness (CASEL, 
2020[21]). 
The TALIS Starting Strong 2018 Conceptual Framework (Sim et al., 2019[22]) included a discussion of 
the extensive body of research on the conceptualisation and measurement of process quality. According 
to the literature, effective pedagogical practices can include interactions that help develop general 
higher-order cognitive skills and interactions that promote domain-specific learning (OECD, 2015b[23]; 
Sylva et al., 2004a[24]; UNESCO, 2013[25]; Vegas et al., 2013[26]; Yoshikawa et al., 2013[27]). In addition, 
high-quality staff-child interactions are characterised by sensitive and consistent emotional responses 
from ECEC staff. These positive staff-child interactions have been found to be associated with the 
development of pro-social behaviour (Johnson et al., 2013[28]) and self-regulation (Williford et al., 
2013[29]) in children. 
A range of pedagogical practices have been identified as contributing to process quality. One example 
is scaffolding: the process by which adults (parents or educators) adjust their interaction with a child to 
ensure that the support they provide is developmentally appropriate (Cazden, 1983[30]; Dorn, 1996[31]; 
Sawyer, 2006[32]). Scaffolding – when integrated with play and embedded in a developmentally 
appropriate curriculum – is a key element of process quality that facilitates the development of children’s 
linguistic, numerical, and social skills, among others (Ciolan, 2013[33]; Moser et al., 2017[34]). 
Incorporating children’s perspectives to inform staff practice and to encourage child-to-child interactions 
is another practice that is characteristic of good process quality (Church and Bateman, 2019[35]; Næsby, 
2019[36]; Vandenbroeck, 2020[37]). It has been found that actively engaging children to make choices 
about their own learning can boost their creativity and their enjoyment of ECEC (Cronin, 2016[38]). 

   27 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Indeed, there is no single practice that constitutes excellent process quality. Regardless of the ECEC 
context (that is, whole group, small group, free play, outdoor time, and routines), research underlines 
the need for ECEC staff to draw on a repertoire of effective strategies, examples of which are discussed 
in the text that follows. 
The skill of adapting activities to the content, type of learning experience, and individual child, as 
informed by a clear learning target, is called intentional teaching (Epstein, 2007[39]; NAEYC/NAECS-
SDE, 2003[40]). Intentional teaching (or intentional pedagogy) has come to be seen as increasingly 
important in ECEC provision since it involves deliberate and purposeful decisions on the part of the 
practitioner, drawing not only on their professional expertise and pedagogical skills but also on their 
understanding of the individual needs of the children in their group. This level of information synthesis 
then allows practitioners to create meaningful and appropriate experiences for children in support of 
learning and well-being (McLaughlin, Aspden and Snyder, 2016[41]; Siraj-Blatchford, 2014[42]). Intentional 
teaching is linked to what is often referred to in later stages of education as ‘differentiated instruction’ – 
that is, teachers’ deliberate adaptation of materials, activities, and assessments to meet students’ 
learning needs and interests (Smale-Jacobse et al., 2019[43]). Both intentional teaching and 
differentiated instruction are underpinned by the assumption that learners can achieve higher levels of 
engagement when, rather than taking a one-size-fits-all approach, practitioners design learning 
processes that account for children’s individual backgrounds, interests, and readiness levels 
(Tomlinson, 2017[44]). 
Literature often draws a distinction between child-centred instruction (characterised by child-initiated 
activities, children’s engagement in problem solving, and inquiry-oriented learning) and didactic 
instruction (characterised by staff-directed activities and planned tasks focused on acquiring and 
practicing academic skills). Both approaches can boost academic skills, but there is some evidence that 
child-centred instruction may be more effective (Huffman and Speer, 2000[45]). The consensus view can 
be characterised as social-constructivist, which stresses the importance of children’s intrinsically 
motivated activity and initiative as the driver of development (McMullen et al., 2005[46]; Pramling-
Samuelsson and Fleer, 2009[47]). 
It is important to note, however, how different cultures may perceive the value of more didactic 
approaches (such as whole-group teaching), as opposed to more constructivist and personalised 
methodologies (such as child-initiated play) – with the latter perceived as being more closely aligned 
with cultural values typically characterised as ‘Western’ (e.g., individualism) (Hu et al., 2015[48]). 
Moreover, it should be stressed that child-centred and didactic instruction are not mutually exclusive 
and can both be integrated in the delivery of ECEC. 
Indeed, the role of ECEC staff is not limited to creating conditions to facilitate self-propelled 
development. ECEC staff are also responsible for developing children’s skills and experiences in various 
competence areas such as language, literacy, numeracy, social skills, and emotional development. In 
doing so, ECEC staff should respect developmental and motivational principles – allowing children, 
where appropriate, to take initiative and to determine their own learning pathways through the 
curriculum, using constructivist, symbolic pretend play and small-group, collaborative work as the main 
vehicles to stimulate development. This approach characterises “developmentally appropriate practice”, 
as coined by Bredekamp (1987[49]). 
Evidence indicates that ECEC curricula designed according to the pedagogical principles of 
developmentally appropriate practice may be especially important for the development of children’s 
cognitive control, self-regulation, and creativity (Diamond and Lee, 2011[50]; McClelland, Acock and 
Morrison, 2006[51]). The development of cognitive control and emotional self-regulation in early childhood 
has been found to be promoted by peer interaction in pretend play (Berk, Mann and Ogan, 2006[52]; 
Bodrova, 2008[53]). Emotional self-regulation skills also appear to be enhanced through socio-dramatic 

28    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
play, with children taking up roles that require imagining others’ state of mind (Elias and Berk, 2002[54]; 
Slot et al., 2017[55]). 
Major advances in cognitive ability that typically occur in early childhood are also known to lay the 
foundations for further learning in other competence areas, such as literacy or numeracy (Hohm et al., 
2007[56]; Lonigan, Burgess and Anthony, 2000[57]). Indeed, a growing body of correlational research 
suggests that the development of executive functioning skills during the early years, such as children’s 
capacity to manipulate items in their working memory, are positively associated with mathematics 
attainment in early childhood education [see, for example: Lan et al. (2011[58]) and McClelland et al. 
(2014[59])]. 
For children under the age of three, research also points to the importance of predictable play-based 
activities and routine care that is provided within a balanced curriculum (Johnson et al., 2019[60]). This 
also involves the use of narrative and storybook reading and informal conversations – both in the context 
of staff-child and child-to-child interactions. While there is evidence on how specific pedagogical 
strategies can be best combined with sensitive, responsive, and warm interactions and relationships to 
promote a young child’s holistic development (Downer, Sabol and Hamre, 2010[61]), evidence specific 
to ECEC provision for children under the age of three is more limited compared to evidence regarding 
children of pre-primary age. 
Another aspect of process quality that reflects the nature of staff-child interactions is classroom or 
playroom climate, which is also known as emotional climate. Classroom or playroom climate (Howes, 
2000[62]; Raver, 2004[63]; Raver, Garner and Smith-Donald, 2007[64]) refers to the nature of staff–child 
relationships and the extent to which these may be characterised by warmth and responsiveness, as 
opposed to anger and harshness (Li Grining et al., 2010[65]). ECEC staff’s levels of job satisfaction, well-
being, stress, and burnout, together with the larger ECEC setting’s organisational climate, may be 
correlated with the classroom or playroom climate (Cumming, 2017[66]). 
Research has demonstrated that a negative classroom or playroom climate tends to adversely affect 
children’s social outcomes (Howes, 2000[62]; Howes et al., 2011[67]; Hughes and Kwok, 2007[68]; NICHD 
Early Child Care Research Network, 2003[69]; Ponitz et al., 2009[70]). In contrast, a cross-country study 
by Wysłowska and Slot (2020[71]) found that, in conjunction with a broad range of stimulating and 
developmentally appropriate activities, a positive and warm classroom or playroom climate was 
beneficial for toddlers’ development and learning. 
In sum, while there is no single conceptualisation of process quality, there are areas of growing 
consensus: for example, the use of a child-centred and developmentally appropriate approach 
(McMullen et al., 2005[46]; Pramling-Samuelsson and Fleer, 2009[47]), the use of intentional teaching 
(McLaughlin, Aspden and Snyder, 2016[41]; Siraj-Blatchford, 2014[42]) or the focus on positive emotional 
climate (Howes, 2000[62]; Howes et al., 2011[67]; Hughes and Kwok, 2007[68]; NICHD Early Child Care 
Research Network, 2003[69]; Ponitz et al., 2009[70]). More broadly, the literature on process quality points 
to a range of appropriate practices that, together, can provide a positive classroom/playroom climate 
and ensure a child-centred, developmentally appropriate approach to children’s development, well-
being, and learning. 
It is important to note, however, that since process quality is typically conceptualised as a 
classroom/playroom-level variable, it is likely to be influenced by factors situated in other spheres of the 
ECEC ecosystem, such as structural quality characteristics and ECEC staff and leader characteristics. 
For instance, high-quality interactions may have greater impact on children when staff-child ratios are 
low, since this provides opportunities for more frequent and sustained interactions (Pianta et al., 
2009[72]). 
There is a strong theoretical basis for expecting that process quality is a key driver of children’s 
development, well-being and learning—accompanied by emerging evidence of a predictive relationship 

   29 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
(Anderson and & Phillips, 2017[73]; Hong et al., 2019[74]; Ulferts, Wolf and Anders, 2019[75]). However, 
rigorous experimental and quasi-experimental studies regarding process quality remain sparse (Sim 
et al., 2019[22]). Many studies that relate measures of process quality to child outcomes—including those 
cited throughout this discussion—are correlational in design. Where evidence is available, the results 
have been mixed (Sim et al., 2019[22]). 
For instance, a review by Burchinal (2018[1]) found that children who experienced higher process quality 
also scored higher on a range of cognitive and socioemotional outcomes. In addition, recent evidence 
suggests there may be a cumulative effect of process quality, with sustained year-on-year exposure to 
high-quality environments having a positive impact on literacy and mathematics outcomes (Carr et al., 
2019[76]). Meta-analytical, longitudinal, and secondary analyses of multiple data points also demonstrate 
a predictive, and possibly lasting, relationship between process quality and learning outcomes 
(Burchinal, Kainz and Cai, 2011[77]; Keys et al., 2013[78]; Hong et al., 2019[74]; Ulferts, Wolf and Anders, 
2019[75]). However, it must be noted that the standardised effect size in these studies is low (where 
significant, ranging between.05 to.1013 for both cognitive and social-emotional outcomes). In addition, 
findings and measures of process quality are not consistent across samples (Keys et al., 2013[78]; Hong 
et al., 2019[74]). 
Small but positive associations between some, but not all, aspects of process quality and child outcomes 
have been found by studies conducted in China (Li et al., 2016[79]), Portugal (Abreu-Lima et al., 2013[80]), 
Chile (Leyva et al., 2015[81]), and Finland (Pakarinen et al., 2011[82]). In contrast, one large and 
representative US study did not find any associations between process quality and child outcomes 
(Burchinal, Zaslow and Tarullo, 2016[83]). 
It must also be noted that a large majority of published evidence regarding process quality draws on 
studies conducted in Western development, well-being, and learning environments (Tobin, 2022[84]; 
Pianta and Hofkens, 2023[8]). The preponderance of evidence from Western ECEC settings may have 
led to a culturally biased understanding of what constitutes ‘good practice’ in ECEC instruction (Tobin, 
2022[84]). In consequence, most instruments for measuring the quality of ECEC provision favour 
practices that are considered acceptable by Western standards (Ishimine and Tayler, 2014[85]). 
Moreover, prevalent understandings of ECEC quality also influence the pre-service training and 
continuous professional development received by ECEC staff across different countries; these, in turn, 
have an impact on staff-child interactions (Campbell-Bar and Bogatić, 2017[86]). 
 Nonetheless, Ishimine and Tayler (2014[85]) recognise that, while process quality instruments 
predominantly originate from Western ECEC settings, these instruments provide some room for 
culturally specific modifications. However, these instruments’ cross-cultural validity—especially when 
incorporating cultural modifications—is, to a large extent, psychometrically untested (Ishimine and 
Tayler, 2014[85]). This issue of cross-cultural validity is made more complex by the variability in how 
ECEC quality is understood, even when accounting only for Western education systems, where 
research is more readily available (Klinkhammer et al., 2017[87]; Tobin, 2022[84]). 
The mixed findings and cultural caveats discussed above suggest that, while the available tools for 
measuring process quality—most of which are based on observational data—may, in some instances, 
be able to capture a predictive relationship, they are not sufficiently developed to robustly capture the 
aspects of process quality that matter most for children’s development, well-being, and learning (Pianta, 
Hamre and Nguyen, 2020[14]). Moreover, existing tools may not always take stock of cultural specificities. 
These issues underscore a need for new and improved tools to assess process quality. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
As a workforce survey, TALIS Starting Strong cannot assess the relationship between process quality 
and child development, well-being, and learning. Nonetheless, it can be used to explore the views and 
practices of ECEC setting staff and leaders regarding various dimensions of process quality, which are 

30    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
in turn known to influence children’s development, well-being, and learning outcomes. TALIS Starting 
Strong 2024 collects a variety of indicators across these dimensions to ensure a sufficiently wide range 
of items. This would allow for a holistic appraisal of process quality within the limits of a workforce survey. 
The relationship between these process quality indicators and various characteristics of the ECEC 
workforce and ECEC settings can also be explored. 
Thus, one key focus for TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is collecting self-reported, proxy measures of 
process quality, as objectively as possible, from leaders and teachers, that allow within- and between-
country comparisons. These comparisons may help explain variations in the reported quality of early 
development, well-being, and learning environments that children experience. 
However, being a survey, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 only captures participants’ responses to questions 
that are intended to capture aspects of process quality. While these responses should have the benefit 
of reflecting long-term patterns in beliefs and practices, responses are dependent on participant-level 
factors like their accuracy of recall and tendency to respond in a socially desirable manner, as well as 
cultural differences in response styles. These are important limitations of the survey’s process quality 
measures. 
As noted earlier, several strategies were employed in TALIS Starting Strong 2018 to obtain assessments 
of process quality, in an effort to minimise potential biases attributable to social desirability, culture, or 
other considerations. These approaches included: (a) collecting information on ECEC staff’s beliefs 
regarding the importance of specific learning objectives; (b) asking ECEC staff respondents about 
practices used by their colleagues at their ECEC setting; (c) asking ECEC staff about how frequently 
they engage in specified practices with their target group; and (d) using situational judgement tasks. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 builds on the strategies listed above and iterates on them, where necessary, 
based on issues encountered in the 2018 cycle. The following strategies continue to be used in TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024: 
• 
Collecting information on the beliefs of ECEC staff respondents regarding the importance 
of specific learning objectives for children. This is based on research indicating an 
association between such beliefs and actual teaching practices (Pianta et al., 2005[6]; Sim 
et al., 2019[22]; Stipek et al., 2001[88]). 
• 
Asking ECEC staff respondents about the frequency of using specific practices with a ‘target 
group’ of children (defined as the group of children they were worked with the most on the 
last working day before the survey date). 
• 
Using concrete vignettes describing everyday situations that staff may encounter in ECEC 
provision (i.e., Situational Judgement Tasks items or SJTs), respondents indicate which of 
the actions listed as options they are likely to take. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 no longer uses questions that ask respondents about the practices of ‘ECEC 
staff at this ECEC centre’, rather than the practices of respondents themselves. These questions are 
asked with respect to their frequency during the previous work week, to encourage staff to think 
concretely about their practices. By collecting data about the respondents’ own practices rather than the 
practices in their setting, it will be possible to link individuals’ practices with their background 
characteristics, such as level and content of education and training programmes. 
  Other strategies to improve the viability of self-reported data on process quality collected by TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024 include: (a) revisiting the response scale used in questions to obtain better 
response variability; (b) reconsidering the placement of process quality questions to make use of more 
concrete reference points (such as the target group or typical working week); and (c) reviewing items to 
ensure a comprehensive set of practices can be captured. 

   31 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Despite these challenges and limitations, developing process quality indicators remains a goal for TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024. These process quality indicators allow for analysing and reporting on interactions 
that are known to influence children’s development, well-being, and learning outcomes. Furthermore, 
over the course of developing the survey instruments through the pilot and field trial, particular attention 
was given to improving these items in terms of their validity and response burden. 
Another key priority was to build upon the initial work on SJTs in the first cycle of the survey. This 
involved consultations with the ECEC QEG to develop SJTs that are both reliable and valid. To address 
the poor reliability and multidimensionality exhibited by the SJTs in TALIS Starting Strong 2018, a 
theory- or construct-based approach became the starting point for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
Following the approach successfully adopted by Aldrup et al. (2020[89]), the CLASS framework was used 
as an initial theoretical basis for designing the SJTs in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. CLASS has been 
extensively validated as a framework and tool for capturing process quality with respect to ECEC 
provision for the relevant age groups (Pianta et al., 2008[13]). Furthermore, it is conceptually aligned with 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024, in that it also measures process quality through staff-child interactions. 
Selecting SJTs for inclusion in the final instruments was based on the SJTs’ psychometric performance 
in the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 Field Trial. Contextual and cultural sensitivities were also considered 
in this selection process, acknowledging that, while the conceptualisation of process quality in CLASS 
is widely used, what counts as ‘best practice’ may differ across TALIS Starting Strong countries. 
 TALIS Starting Strong 2024 differentiates between global and domain-specific process quality, as 
defined by Ulferts et al. (2019[75]). Global process quality captures general aspects of physical and 
emotional care and support, instruction, and cognitive stimulation experienced by children, which aim to 
support their global development, well-being, and learning. Domain-specific process quality pertains to 
care, support, instruction, and cognitive stimulation that targets development in specific learning areas, 
such as early literacy or numeracy. Often these processes will overlap, even if they are intended to 
serve different purposes by staff. 
As with TALIS Starting Strong 2018, the 2024 cycle aims to capture indicators of process quality, or 
more specifically indicators of the quality of staff-child interaction, across two domains and eight sub-
domains: 
a) practices aimed at supporting children’s global development, well-being, and learning: 
• 
Adapting practices to individual children’s needs and interests 
• 
Supporting classroom/playgroup management 
• 
Supporting play and exploration 
b) Practices aimed at facilitating domain-specific development: 
• 
Supporting language 
• 
Supporting early literacy 
• 
Supporting early numeracy 
• 
Supporting pro-social behaviour 
• 
Supporting emotional development and well-being 
The identified domains do not represent the universe of all possible process quality domains, however, 
and the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 conceptualisation of process quality may differ from 
conceptualisations used in other studies. 
Within content area analyses 
As with TALIS Starting Strong 2018, there is a need to understand within- and between-country 
heterogeneity of pedagogical practices and play. Analyses of between-country heterogeneity will 

32    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
continue to be conditional on measurement comparability. Detailed analyses of between-country 
differences in the way process quality is operationalised in the classroom/playroom will inform an 
understanding of indicators that are more universal across countries, and those that may be more 
culturally-relative. 
Cross-content area analyses 
Indicators related to the quality of staff-child interactions are also a main area of interest in TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024. Analyses within and between countries and jurisdictions can shed light on 
associations between process quality and: (a) structural quality indicators at setting and 
classroom/playroom level; (b) resources available to ECEC settings (e.g., funding and funding 
structures); (c) staff and leader characteristics, their professional development, and their professional 
beliefs; (d) staff and leader well-being; and (e) diversity, equity, and inclusion in ECEC settings. 
Monitoring and assessment of children’s development, well-being, and learning 
Monitoring and assessment are closely related terms that refer to how early childhood professionals 
gain an understanding of children’s development, well-being, and learning. However, while closely 
related, they are distinct processes. On one hand, monitoring tracks children’s learning process and 
identifies areas where they need support to help them meet developmentally appropriate objectives. On 
the other hand, assessment aims to determine whether children reach specific learning and 
development objectives. 
 TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is particularly well-suited to capture information related to monitoring and 
assessment of children’s development, well-being, and learning. The survey collects information on how 
staff use monitoring and assessment data for ECEC provision. In addition, it asks respondents whether 
monitoring and assessment data is shared across ECEC staff to discuss children’s development, well-
being, and learning needs. It also asks whether this information is shared with staff in subsequent 
educational levels to ensure smooth transitions of children across ECEC stages or onto primary 
education. 
Given the wide range of monitoring and assessment methods that can exist in ECEC settings, it is 
important that staff have the knowledge and skills to use these methods correctly and appropriately. As 
such, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 captures information related to the training and professional 
development received by ECEC staff on monitoring and assessing children’s development. In addition, 
the survey asks ECEC staff about their sense of self-efficacy when it comes to using monitoring and 
assessment tools. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Research has shown that ECEC staff who know children’s level of development demonstrate better 
pedagogical practices (Cadima et al., 2020[90]). Monitoring and assessing children’s development, well-
being, and learning can play an important role in improving staff practices and service provision, and 
thus enhance children’s development (Litjens, 2013[91]). For instance, information regarding children’s 
development in specific skill areas helps practitioners adjust their practices to children’s individual needs 
(Barblett and Maloney, 2010[92]) and adapt curricula and standards to meet said needs (Litjens, 2013[91]). 
In fact, Yoshikawa et al. (2013[27]) argue that most successful curricula take stock of findings from the 
monitoring and assessment of children’s progress. 
There is also a need for developmentally appropriate methods and tools (Meisels and Atkins-Burnett, 
2000[93]; Sattler, 1998[94]) that are designed to identify children’s development needs, abilities, and skills, 
according to their age (Barnett, Ayers and Francis, 2014[95]; Waterman et al., 2012[96]). In addition, 

   33 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
monitoring and assessment methods for ECEC should be enjoyable, not stressful for children, and be 
grounded on children’s interests (Copple and Bredekamp, 2009[97]; Meisels and Atkins-Burnett, 2000[93]). 
Staff competence in using the results of monitoring and assessment for different purposes and in 
deploying a variety of developmentally appropriate monitoring and assessment tools is therefore critical 
(Iqbal, Saleem and Arshad, 2020[98]). Staff who have sufficient knowledge and skills to use assessment 
in ECEC settings are better at adapting their teaching to the specific developmental needs of the child 
(Iqbal, Saleem and Arshad, 2020[98]). Given this, training on monitoring and assessment becomes an 
important consideration. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2018 results showed that approximately 90% of surveyed staff across 
participating countries received some training on monitoring and documenting children’s development, 
well-being, and learning – be that during their pre-service training, in-service training, or both (OECD, 
2020b[99]). Initial preparation and continuing professional development on monitoring and assessment 
can also shape staff views on this area. For instance, a study with an Israeli sample showed that even 
a basic assessment course changed teachers’ conceptions about the role of assessment for improving 
learning and teaching (Levy-Vered and Alhija, 2018[100]). 
Various developmental areas, learning objectives, and aspects of well-being can and should be included 
in monitoring or assessment, such as: language and literacy; numeracy and mathematics; science; 
digital literacy; socioemotional development; motor development; self-care and health; practical life 
skills; autonomy; creativity; and well-being, among others (Barblett and Maloney, 2010[92]; Raver, 
2002[101]; Snow and van Hemel, 2008[102]). As a result, it becomes important to ensure developmentally 
appropriate monitoring and assessment practices that take a holistic approach – that is, practices that 
are not limited to measuring narrow cognitive domains - see also Barnett, Ayers, & Francis (2014[95]). 
For the Starting Strong IV report (OECD, 2015b[23]), the OECD surveyed 21 countries or jurisdictions on 
the use of instruments for monitoring child development. The findings published in this report revealed 
that, while monitoring and assessing child development, well-being, and learning is a mostly internal 
process and is often linked to staff- and setting-level practices, external agencies can play a role in this 
process. In addition, national, regional, or local policies influence monitoring and assessment. 
The OECD (2015b[23]) Starting Strong IV report categorises frequently used assessment tools into three 
categories: 
1. Direct assessments, such as standardised assessments and screening instruments, which 
are intended to measure children’s knowledge, skills, and aptitudes Approaches to 
comprehensive/continuous child supports; 
2. Narrative assessments, such as storytelling and portfolios, which describe children’s 
development through narratives or stories based on artefacts produced by the child 
3. Observational methods, such as rating scales and checklists 
Monitoring and assessment can either be formative or summative. Formative monitoring or assessment 
pertains to formal and informal procedures conducted by ECEC staff in their day-to-day work. This can 
take the form of observation, task-based activities, or unstructured interviews (Brodie, 2013[103]). On one 
hand, formative assessment informs adjustments made by ECEC staff to their instructional approach or 
to the environment to better support children’s development, well-being, and learning. On the other 
hand, summative monitoring or assessment indicates the child’s current level of development, well-
being, and learning based on information gathered from a range of sources. In summative assessment, 
the child’s progress is determined by comparing current levels of knowledge and ability against an 
established standard or benchmark. 
However, while formative and summative assessment serve distinct purposes, they are not mutually 
exclusive and are in fact often used alongside each other. After all, researchers and practitioners 

34    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
recommend assessment systems that use multiple, ongoing methods (NAEYC/NAECS-SDE, 2003[40]; 
Shepard, Kagan and Wurtz, 1998[104]). 
Nevertheless, it is worth discussing emerging concerns about the risks of using summative assessments 
in an ECEC context. ECEC staff in many countries are concerned about the potential misuse of 
summative methods. For instance, misuse may lead to the ‘schoolification’ of ECEC pedagogy, where 
placing a ‘score’ on children’s learning becomes the main priority, hence taking focus away from a child’s 
holistic well-being and development (Alcock and Haggerty, 2013[105]; Lazzari and Vandenbroeck, 
2013[106]). ECEC staff also expressed concern about using assessment to delay or deny school entry, 
since doing so risks a child being labelled as a ‘failure’ early in their school career; this could adversely 
affect a child’s development. 
Formative assessment can be deliberate and organised (Guddemi and Case, 2004[107]). A study in the 
United States measured practices and environments to promote children’s development in literacy and 
language. This study found the following conditions to have a positive effect on the quality of ECEC 
practices: (a) when a curriculum-based child assessment tool is used; (b) when the development of 
portfolios is aligned with the federal programme for early learning; and (c) when child assessment 
information is integrated into instructional planning (Hallam et al., 2007[108]). 
However, since formative evaluation is embedded in ongoing pedagogical activities, it is not typically 
identified as a separate instructional activity in the same way as summative assessment (for example, 
in the form of an end-of-term examination). ECEC staff have traditionally used formative assessment 
through informal methods, such as authentic, naturalistic observations, narrative and anecdotal records, 
and portfolios (Copple and Bredekamp, 2009[97]; Meisels and Atkins-Burnett, 2000[93]). There is some 
evidence of a positive relationship between the use of non-formal monitoring mechanisms, such as 
observation, documentation, portfolios or narrative assessments and positive child development, well-
being and learning when these are used to inform practice (Bagnato, 2005[109]; Grisham-Brown, 
2008[110]; Meisels, 2003[111]). 
According to the OECD (2015b[23]) Starting Strong IV report, the monitoring of child development through 
observations and narrative assessments is more common and comprehensive than direct assessments. 
The most prevalent areas covered by observation-based assessments are literacy, numeracy, and 
teacher-rated social-emotional adjustment, while self-regulation and higher-order cognitive skills are 
largely absent from monitoring and assessment mechanisms (Burchinal, 2018[1]). On the other hand, 
direct assessments, in many jurisdictions, tend to cover a narrower set of domains than observations 
and narrative assessments. More than half of the surveyed jurisdictions apply direct assessments that 
often focus on skills such as language and literacy, health development, socio-emotional skills, and 
motor skills (OECD, 2015b[23]). 
It is also worth discussing cultural considerations regarding the use of monitoring and assessment in 
ECEC. Culture guides behaviour, and as such, differences in cultural values may influence staff 
opinions, beliefs, and practices regarding child monitoring and assessment (Grieve, 1992[112]). 
Therefore, monitoring and assessing child development should be sensitive to cultural values and beliefs 
about child development; involving family and community members in this process can help to ensure 
that cultural context is considered (Broekhuizen et al., 2015[113]; Espinosa and López, 2007[114]; Oliver 
et al., 2011[115]). This is also stressed in the OECD Network on ECEC’s (2015[116]) document, 
‘Early Learning and Development: Common Understandings’. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
The main indicators on the monitoring and assessment of children’s development, well-being, and 
learning are:

TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
• 
Content of initial education regarding monitoring and assessment 
• 
Content of professional development and need for further development regarding 
monitoring and assessment 
• 
Self-efficacy regarding the monitoring and assessment of children 
• 
Staff use of monitoring and assessment 
• 
Time spent on the monitoring and assessment of children 
•

•

Cross-content area analyses 
Indicators related to the monitoring and assessment of children’s development, well-being, and learning 
will be key predictor variables in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. Analyses will also likely examine how 
monitoring and assessment are influenced by policies and structural quality, using the corresponding 
survey questions, items, and measures. 
Measures of child monitoring and assessment provide an opportunity to address research issues such 
as: 
1. Monitoring or assessment in relation to continuing professional development, initial training, 
culture, and ideological beliefs. 
2. Links between child monitoring and assessment, process quality, and pedagogical 
practices. 
3. ECEC staff beliefs regarding monitoring and assessment, including how prepared they feel 
to conduct assessments. 
 
Professional beliefs about children’s development, well-being and learning 
ECEC staff beliefs regarding young children’s development, well-being, and learning influence their 
pedagogical approach and practice, which in turn, influences children’s outcomes (Staub and Stern, 
2002[117]; Blömeke, 2017[118]). For instance, ECEC staff beliefs regarding the value of direct instruction 
(i.e., teacher-centred practices) with young children are likely to influence the extent to which they use 
the said approach. 
ECEC staff beliefs and practices may be shaped by various factors. For instance, situational factors, 
such as the degree of staff independence or management and group sizes, affect the nature of the 
relationship between beliefs and practices (König and Pflanzl, 2016[119]; König and Rothland, 2013[120]). 
The pre-service and in-service education and training received by ECEC staff can also affect ECEC 
beliefs and practices (König, 2012[121]). Cultural, historical, and familial contexts also exert an influence 
(König, 2012[121]). 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Link between beliefs and practice 
Beliefs of ECEC staff regarding young children’s development, well-being, and learning directly shape 
their practices (Charlesworth et al., 1991[122]; Kagan, Tarrant and Kauerz, 2008[123]; Pianta et al., 2005[6]; 
Stipek and Byler, 1997[124]; Stipek et al., 2001[88]). In fact, Pianta and colleagues (2005[6]) found that, 

36    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
even after adjusting for staff experience or training and structural factors (such as staff-child ratio), ECEC 
staff beliefs about children had the strongest association with observed pedagogical quality. 
However, more recent studies have found the association between pedagogical beliefs and practices in 
ECEC to be less clear [see, for example: Lam (2018[125]); Gerde et al. (2019[126]); Ata-Akturk and Sevimli-
Celik (2020[127])]. For instance, a study in Singapore conducted by Ting Tan and Rao (2017[128]) found 
that, although staff tended to report having child-centred rather than teacher-centred beliefs, their actual 
practice was largely teacher-directed, didactic, and focused on children acquiring academic skills. 
Similarly, in a study with a Norwegian sample, Nilsen (2021[129]) found that ECEC staff unanimously 
acknowledged the importance of having play materials available for children and believe that play 
materials contribute to children’s learning and development. Notwithstanding, ECEC staff would still take 
play materials out of children's reach due to practical considerations. The studies cited above indicate 
how pedagogical beliefs might not always reflect in ECEC staff practices. 
Factors that influence beliefs 
Some studies suggest that staff competencies and qualifications seem to be closely associated with 
pedagogical beliefs (Wieduwilt, Lehrl and Anders, 2021[130]). Research from different countries has 
found that historical narratives and cultural context – which can vary even across regions within the 
same country (Matsumoto and Tsuneda, 2019[131]) – are also important influences on ECEC staff. 
The beliefs and practices of ECEC staff can also be influenced by the characteristics of the children and 
families with whom they work. For example, children’s socio-economic status (SES) has been found to 
be related to ECEC staff practices (Lee and Ginsburg, 2007a[132]; Stipek and Byler, 1997[124]). Children 
from low-SES backgrounds are often behind their more affluent peers in many areas, and awareness 
of this disparity may influence the beliefs and practices of ECEC staff who serve children from 
disadvantaged backgrounds. For example, ECEC staff working with low-SES children rate memorising 
facts and rote tasks (procedural knowledge) as more important pedagogical goals than problem-solving 
and tasks involving reasoning [conceptual knowledge; Stipek & Byler, (1997[124])]. ECEC staff who work 
with low-SES children also tend to prioritise the acquisition of more basic skills, in comparison to their 
counterparts who work with middle-SES children (Stipek and Byler, 1997[124]). 
In another study, ECEC staff working with low-SES children believed that children should engage in 
mathematics activities in preparation for kindergarten (i.e., the year before formal school), even if they 
initially showed little or no interest (Lee and Ginsburg, 2007b[133]). Conversely, ECEC staff working with 
middle-SES children were more likely to express that activities should be child-focused and child-
initiated, and they tended to emphasise children’s social-emotional development (Lee, 2006[134]; Lee 
and Ginsburg, 2007b[133]). Lee and Ginsburg (2007b[133]) hypothesised this was because ECEC staff 
believed that middle-SES parents already provided their children with significant academic support at 
home. 
Changing beliefs 
Trying to change pedagogy without considering pedagogical beliefs may prompt resistance (Lee and 
Ginsburg, 2007b[133]; Ryan and Grieshaber, 2004[135]). As such, efforts to change pedagogical practices 
must consider how staff perceive their role with young children, as well as their beliefs about the purpose 
of ECEC settings. In addition, some pedagogical beliefs have been found to be more malleable than 
others (Di Santo, Timmons and Lenis, 2017[136]). For instance, Di Santo and colleagues (2017[136]) 
reported that beliefs about behaviour management were more malleable to change, while beliefs about 
classroom or playroom practice and beliefs about children tended to be stable. Hence, changing beliefs 
is a relatively complex endeavour. 

   37 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Furthermore, research has shown that ECEC staff views are often established even before they receive 
initial training (Isikoglu, 2008[137]; La Paro et al., 2009[138]; Vartuli and Rohs, 2009[139]). Multiple factors, 
including family, friends, religion, media, and ECEC staff’s own educational experiences, influence 
beliefs about pedagogy and practice (Aldemir and Sezer, 2009[140]). Nonetheless, opportunities to learn 
appropriate pedagogy in a given subject can mediate staff beliefs. For instance, one study found that 
knowledge of mathematics and opportunities to learn mathematics pedagogy were significantly related 
to beliefs (Blömeke, Dunekacke and Jenßen, 2017[141]). This suggests that, while beliefs may indeed 
develop well before ECEC staff undergo initial training, professional development can still play a role in 
changing practitioners’ beliefs and practices. 
Another study found a link between ECEC staff’s perceived need for professional development in 
science, technology, maths, and engineering (STEM), their pedagogical beliefs about STEM, and their 
sense of self-efficacy (Chen, Huang and Wu, 2021[142]). The same study found STEM pedagogical 
beliefs to mediate the association between self-efficacy and perceived need for professional 
development, suggesting that beliefs can motivate staff to address the difficulties they face in delivering 
ECEC. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
Capturing data on professional beliefs is relevant from a policy perspective, given how beliefs can 
influence various aspects of process quality. In addition, obtaining this data through TALIS Starting 
Strong 2024 provides an opportunity to explore cross-country and cross-cultural differences in 
professional beliefs. 
However, questions on beliefs need to be carefully considered to avoid social desirability bias. Socially 
desirable responding may be particularly marked in ECEC staff, since there are often very public 
discourses about what young children should be and are able to do. The socially acceptable discourse 
may differ from one’s beliefs; for instance, while child-centric models are popular, an ECEC practitioner’s 
personal beliefs may be that teaching should be adult-directed. However, when reporting their beliefs, 
the same practitioner might indicate an alignment with child-centric ECEC, since they know that this is 
the most socially accepted answer. 
Indicators and dimensions concerned with professional beliefs about children’s development, well-being 
and learning can be summarised as follows: 
• 
Beliefs about enhancing the development of children’s abilities and skills, and 
• 
Beliefs about diversity, equity, and inclusion practices 
Cross-content area analyses 
Information on professional beliefs – in conjunction with information on process quality and related 
content areas – may give insight into ECEC staff’s education, training, and professional development 
needs. Capturing data on ECEC staff’s professional beliefs about children’s development, well-being, 
and learning can also help answer research questions concerning: 
• 
The relationship between professional beliefs and staff background (e.g., previous 
education or professional development); 
• 
Cultural differences in professional beliefs and how these may influence patterns of 
professional development in ECEC across countries and jurisdictions; 
• 
Professional beliefs on how to develop children’s skills for life in the 21st century; 

38    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
• 
The association of professional beliefs with staff self-efficacy, ECEC setting climate, job 
satisfaction, pedagogical practices, and process quality. 
Self-efficacy 
The concept of self-efficacy was introduced by Bandura (1977[143]), who defined it as a belief in one’s 
own ability to perform the actions needed to attain a given goal. From an ECEC standpoint, this 
corresponds to the degree to which staff feel comfortable and confident in their ability to perform their 
job (OECD, 2020b[99]). While there has been substantial research on self-efficacy among teachers of 
school-aged children, studies regarding professional self-efficacy within the ECEC context are limited. 
TALIS Starting Strong hence makes a systematic, large-scale effort to document ECEC staff and leader 
self-efficacy. 
In TALIS Starting Strong, self-efficacy refers to ECEC staff’s own beliefs and judgements about their 
own ability to promote children’s development, well-being, and learning. Bandura (1977[143]) clarified 
that, while self-efficacy beliefs may be influenced by external factors and obstacles, self-efficacy is first 
and foremost a subjective evaluation of one’s own capability. Thus, individuals within the same 
environment or context – be it a school, country, or educational system – may have different levels of 
self-efficacy. This conceptualisation comprises elements of self-efficacy theory and is also informed by 
research on effective instruction. 
A positive sense of self-efficacy can lead to improvements in practice and job satisfaction – both of 
which can, in turn, bolster children’s development, well-being, and learning (Armor, 1976[144]; Jeon, 
Buettner and Grant, 2018[145]; Rotter, 1966[146]; Skaalvik and Skaalvik, 2007[147]). As such, capturing data 
on ECEC staff and leader self-efficacy is a matter of policy interest. TALIS Starting Strong can provide 
this data. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Tschannen-Moran and Hoy (2001[148]) and Skaalvik and Skaalvik (2010[149]) identified six aspects of 
educators’ self-efficacy. Combining and adapting their conceptualisations of educators’ self-efficacy to 
an ECEC context, self-efficacy can therefore be conceived as spanning: (i) pedagogical practices; (ii) 
ability to adapt practice to children’s individual needs; (iii) promoting children engagement in and 
excitement for learning; (iv) maintaining a well-organized and ordered environment (e.g., low noise 
levels) in the classroom or playroom; (v) cooperating with colleagues and parents; and (vi) coping with 
change. It is also important to note that self-efficacy can develop over time and with experience, 
especially as teachers’ skills improve (Tschannen-Moran and Hoy, 2001[148]). 
In a review of research, Jerald (2007[150]) found that some teacher behaviours appeared to be related to 
their sense of self-efficacy. Teachers with a stronger sense of self-efficacy: 
• 
Tend to exhibit greater levels of planning and organisation. 
• 
Are more open to new ideas and more willing to experiment with new methods to better 
meet the needs of their students. 
• 
Are more persistent and resilient when things do not go smoothly. 
• 
Are less critical of students when they make errors. 
• 
Are less inclined to refer a difficult student to special education. 
Self-efficacy beliefs are context-specific and connected to instructional capabilities and tasks 
(Tschannen-Moran and Hoy, 2001[148]), (Klassen et al., 2011[151]; Malinen et al., 2013[152]). For instance, 
Yada and colleagues (2019[153]) found differences in the sources of self-efficacy for Japanese and 
Finnish teachers. The sources of self-efficacy considered in their study1 explained only 15% of the 
variance in Japanese teachers’ self-efficacy ratings, while it accounted for 54% in the Finnish teachers’ 

   39 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
ratings. This difference could be at least partly explained by cultural factors, such as the de-emphasising 
of personal accomplishments in Eastern ECEC settings (Yada et al., 2019[153]). 
Studies have found various correlates of self-efficacy in an educational context. The Job Demands-
Resources (JD-R) model explains how both contextual and individual factors can influence work 
performance, well-being and teacher self-efficacy, although the direction of the effect could not be 
established (Bakker and Demerouti, 2007[154]). For instance, Huang, Yin, and Lv (2019[155]) found that 
the emotional job demand of teaching is positively correlated to self-efficacy and well-being in a Hong 
Kong sample. Lipscomb and colleagues (2021[156]) observed that, after controlling for teachers’s 
education level and professional development, self-efficacy and professional support predicted 55% of 
the variance in work engagement. An earlier study, conducted by Albridge and Fraser (2016[157]), yielded 
similar results, demonstrating how teacher self-efficacy and job satisfaction were related and were both 
connected to school climate. There may also be gender differences, with Klassen and Chiu (2010[158]) 
reporting that female educators expressed weaker feelings of self-efficacy in the way of classroom 
management. 
While there is substantial research on teachers’ self-efficacy, comparatively little is known about the 
self-efficacy beliefs of ECEC staff. One pioneering study examining self-efficacy in ECEC was 
conducted by Justice and colleagues (2008[159]), using an adapted version of the Teacher Self-Efficacy 
Scale (Bandura, 1997[160]). They found that ECEC staff reported having generally high self-efficacy when 
it comes to facilitating children’s learning. Guo, Kaderavek, and colleagues (2011[161]) – also using 
Bandura’s (1997[160]) TSES – and Brown (2005[162]), using Tschannen-Moran and Hoy’s (2001[148]) 
Teachers’ Sense of Efficacy Scale, found similar results. 
However, ECEC staff expressed low to moderate levels of self-efficacy in terms of their influence on 
administrative issues, such as classroom/playroom size and composition (Guo et al., 2012[163]; McGinty, 
Justice and Rimm-Kaufman, 2008[164]). This may be due to ECEC staff believing that policy-related 
decisions, such as class size, are made at the level of setting administration, where they feel they have 
no influence. It is worth noting, however, that the self-efficacy studies above were all conducted with an 
American sample. 
Taken together, these studies’ findings indicate that, while ECEC staff in the United States are confident 
in their abilities to motivate and engage young children, manage children’s behaviour, and use effective 
instructional strategies, they feel they have much less control over administrative aspects of ECEC 
provision, which affect structural quality. 
Further research in the United States by Guo and colleagues (2010[165]; 2012[163]) found that ECEC staff 
self-efficacy – by way of its association with improved process quality in staff-child interactions – was 
linked to gains in children's language development. Earlier studies yielded similar results [see, for 
example: Sylva et al. (2004a[24]); Connor et al. (2005[166]); Mashburn et al. (2008[4])]. In other words, the 
quality of staff-child interactions was found to mediate the relationship between ECEC staff self-efficacy 
and children's learning. 
Certain teacher-level characteristics also correlate with self-efficacy. For instance, Bullock and 
colleagues (2015[167]) demonstrated a positive association between years of teaching experience and 
classroom/playroom management self-efficacy. The study also found that ECEC staff’s level of 
extraversion and openness to experience predicted their classroom/playroom management self-
efficacy, beyond years of teaching experience. Another study with pre-service ECEC teachers in Türkiye 
found that participants who reported themselves as being ‘competent’ or ‘moderately competent’ with 
parental involvement in ECEC provision also tended to have higher levels of self-efficacy in this area 
(Alaçam and Olgan, 2017[168]). 
TALIS Starting Strong 2018 is one of few studies that provide insight into ECEC staff’s self-efficacy 
across a range of countries, and its results showed varying levels of self-efficacy across countries and 

40    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
content areas. Broadly speaking, ECEC staff report lower levels of self-efficacy when it comes to working 
with diverse groups of children and expressed a need for more training in this area (Danmarks 
Evalueringsinstitut, 2020[169]; Gjerustad, Hjetland and Opheim, 2019[170]; OECD, 2020b[99]). 
Compared to other aspects of their job, ECEC staff surveyed in TALIS Starting Strong 2018 also felt 
less confident about using digital technology (OECD, 2020b[99]). Other studies generated similar results: 
many ECEC staff lack knowledge and confidence in using online tools in developmentally appropriate 
ways (Marklund, 2019[171]; Murcia, Campbell and Aranda, 2018[172]). 
Therefore, it is of interest for countries to develop policies that would help raise the skills and confidence 
of ECEC staff in the aforementioned areas (OECD, 2020b[99]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024’s questions regarding staff self-efficacy can describe the extent to which 
ECEC staff feel capable of performing different aspects of their role. They can also be used to identify 
differences in ECEC staff self-efficacy across cultures, countries and jurisdictions, and educational 
systems. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 conceptualises staff self-efficacy as encompassing the full range of 
practices and responsibilities that come with being a staff member in an ECEC setting. Hence, questions 
about staff self-efficacy refer to the following areas, among others: 
• 
Self-efficacy regarding staff-child interaction. 
• 
Self-efficacy regarding the monitoring and assessment of children. 
• 
Self-efficacy regarding facilitating children development, well-being and learning. 
• 
Self-efficacy relating to diversity, equity, and inclusion practices. 
• 
Self-efficacy regarding use of digital technologies. 
Cross-content area analyses 
It would be possible to examine the relationship of ECEC staff self-efficacy with other indicators, such 
as: (a) pedagogical practices; (b) prior education and training; (c) professional development; (d) well-
being; (e) organisational climate; and (f) individual background characteristics, such as age, gender, 
and level of professional experience. Understanding these associations can potentially inform 
interventions that aim to strengthen the self-efficacy of ECEC staff. 
Staff and leader characteristics 
Staff and leader demographic and education background 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 collects information about staff and leaders’ backgrounds, such as gender, 
age, work experience and formal education prior to working at their ECEC setting. Besides providing a 
valuable description of ECEC settings and systems, data on staff and leaders’ backgrounds can also be 
used to explore how these characteristics may relate to other indicators (e.g., self-efficacy, well-being, 
and professional beliefs). Furthermore, data on staff and leaders’ background characteristics lends 
context to the survey data on other content areas and indicators. 
This sub-section explores how ECEC staff and leaders’ background characteristics, including their prior 
formal education, relate to other content areas and indicators of the survey. In addition, this sub-section 

   41 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
discusses how practitioners’ background characteristics relate to ECEC quality and children’s 
development, well-being, and learning outcomes. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
There are multiple pathways into ECEC work and the structure, content, and emphasis of initial 
education vary between and within countries (OECD, 2012a[173]). Different regulations for the ECEC 
sector mean there can be large variation across initial training programmes for ECEC practitioners, 
compared to counterpart programmes in primary education (Pardo and Adlerstein, 2016[174]). 
Characteristics of initial education and training (level and length/duration) 
The characteristics of ECEC practitioners’ initial education and training is a matter of policy interest, as 
demonstrated by efforts to professionalise the ECEC workforce. However, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 
found that the proportion of staff who received prior education on working with children varies widely 
across countries, ranging from 64% of ECEC staff in Iceland, to 97% in Germany (OECD, 2020b[99]). 
The characteristics of initial training for ECEC staff, such as the duration and the educational level at 
which the qualification is obtained, also vary by staff category and across countries (Bertram et al., 
2016[175]; Cobano, Llorent and Navarro, 2020[176]; OECD, 2012a[173]; Pardo and Adlerstein, 2016[174]). 
Initial training for ECEC practitioners ranges from a programme held at a secondary education institution 
to master’s degree programmes obtained at university (OECD, 2012a[173]; 2020b[99]). In some countries, 
the level of qualification obtained may vary depending on staff category – that is, teachers and assistants 
might have different qualifications (OECD, 2019c[177]). It is also worth noting that, as a result of the 
previously mentioned professionalisation efforts, countries like Brazil and Chile are abolishing ECEC 
training programmes at the secondary level (Pardo and Adlerstein, 2016[174]). Short-cycle higher 
education ECEC staff preparation programmes, which last two years rather than the three or four years 
of a typical university degree, also exist in certain countries; these were often put in place to attract 
larger numbers of prospective qualified ECEC practitioners, hence addressing shortages (Carver-
Thomas and Darling-Hammond, 2017[178]). 
 Evidence suggests that there are positive associations between having ECEC-specific content or a 
specialisation in early childhood as part of initial training, the length of training and the type of practices 
that staff deploy (OECD, 2018b[10]; Sim et al., 2019[22]). Although many countries have established 
national standards of required competencies, the actual share of ECEC staff trained to these standards 
varies. For example, ECEC staff qualifications in the US range from high school equivalents (or even 
lower for home-based providers) to advanced degrees (Pianta, Hamre and Nguyen, 2020[14]). 
Qualifications of ECEC practitioners vary similarly across European countries, despite the European 
Union’s efforts to establish common criteria. For instance, a study of ECEC preparation programmes in 
England, France, and Spain found that ECEC practitioners across the three countries had different 
levels of qualification, and broadly speaking, the qualifications required of ECEC practitioners were lower 
than what was required of practitioners at other levels of education (Cobano, Llorent and Navarro, 
2020[176]). 
Monitoring in this area is poor in many countries (UNESCO, 2015[179]), and in some countries the 
pressure of enrolment surges (e.g., with the introduction of compulsory education) has lowered training 
and hiring standards. Formal or informal policies supporting the recruitment of staff from ethnic minorities 
or other disadvantaged groups are enforced in many countries, but staff often lack the required training 
to meet national standards (UNESCO, 2015[179]). Furthermore, some countries are contending with 
rapidly increasing enrolment rates (due to the introduction of compulsory ECEC, for instance), which 
has, in some cases, prompted a lowering of training and hiring standards (UNESCO, 2015[179]). 

42    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Pedagogy and content of pre-service education and training 
There is a lack of systematised information regarding the pedagogy, content, and mode of delivery of 
pre-service education and training for ECEC practitioners. Research on the content and quality of ECEC 
preparation programmes also remains limited (Early et al., 2007[180]; Fukkink and Lont, 2007[181]). TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024 provides an opportunity to fill in this gap. 
Pre-service education has the potential to create a common understanding of good practices across 
ECEC staff (OECD, 2019c[177]). The knowledge base that should be included in initial preparation 
programmes for ECEC staff is broad and typically considered to include areas such as: child 
development, curriculum content, pedagogy, disciplinary content (mathematics, language and literacy, 
science, social studies, and arts), psychology, and play. In addition, the following content areas are 
becoming increasingly important: (a) working with diverse groups of children (Vandenbroeck, 2020[37]); 
(b) attending to children’s holistic well-being (OECD, 2019c[177]); (c) effectively engaging families and 
communicating with other educational and non-educational institutions (Haslip and Gullo, 2018[182]; 
Schleicher, 2019[183]); and (d) using digital technologies in developmentally appropriate ways 
(Kalogiannakis and Papadakis, 2019[184]). 
There is also growing interest in providing pre-service trainees with opportunities to rehearse teaching 
practices in real classrooms or playrooms, accompanied by expert teachers; these rehearsal 
opportunities may also be video-recorded and analysed to facilitate the learning of pre-service trainees 
(Ball et al., 2009[185]; Grossman, 2018[186]; Grossman et al., 2009[187]; Lampert, 2010[188]). However, while 
teaching practicum forms part of many pre-service training programmes, the duration of the practicum 
varies across countries. 
Some countries have defined a minimum number of days of practicum required to graduate. For 
example, Norway and Sweden currently require students to complete 100 days of practical placement 
while Denmark requires one year (OECD, 2019a[189]). In Spain, the minimum amount of time trainees 
must spend in the classroom or playroom, at the time of writing, varies between 400 and 600 hours, 
depending on the qualification being pursued (OECD, 2019a[189]). 
The role of internship schools – or university-school partnerships - during initial ECEC training is also 
worth exploring (Zhang, Admiraal and Saab, 2020[190]). Online degrees have also emerged, offering 
ease of access and more flexible education formats (Gardner et al., 2019[191]; Workman, Guernsey and 
Mead, 2018[192]). But regardless of the modality (that is, onsite or online), practical work remains a 
common requirement of most ECEC programmes. 
However, the content of initial ECEC training has not been systematically examined, and this is made 
difficult by the wide range of training pathways into ECEC work. Between - and within-country variation 
in the content of ECEC training programmes, as shown by the results of TALIS Starting Strong 2018, 
adds further complexity. For instance, in a recent study of 14 Latin American countries, Pardo and 
Adlerstein (2016[174]) found that only two countries had national guidelines on curriculum for ECEC pre-
service programmes. In a UK-based survey by Hadfield and Jopling (2012[193]), about 23% of the trained 
ECEC professionals surveyed indicated that their degree had no content that was specifically relevant 
to ECEC, while 64% stated that their degree had directly relevant content. Furthermore, relevance of 
ECEC training was higher among UK practitioners in child-care settings or other local ECEC settings, 
compared to their counterparts in school-based and home-based settings (Hadfield and Jopling, 
2012[193]). 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 aims to contribute towards mapping the various content areas and 
programme components of pre-service ECEC education across different countries. 

   43 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Effectiveness of ECEC pre-service education and training 
Evidence supports the importance of ECEC staff with the specific content knowledge relevant for 
working with young children. Studies have shown that children in ECEC settings whose ECEC staff 
possess knowledge and understanding of child development showed better development, well-being, 
and learning outcomes (Hanno et al., 2020[194]; Naudeau et al., 2011[195]; Siraj-Blatchford et al., 
2003[196]). Furthermore, having a credential in early childhood education has been generally linked to 
higher overall quality in ECEC settings (Manning et al., 2017[197]; Torquati, Raikes and Huddleston-
Casas, 2007[198]). However, the level of education at which the ECEC qualification was obtained, as well 
as the duration of the initial training received, also appears to influence the quality of ECEC provision. 
Various studies have shown higher staff education to be a moderate to strong predictor of higher-quality 
ECEC classroom/playroom environments and higher-quality ECEC staff-child interactions, both of which 
are linked to improved child outcomes (Early et al., 2007[180]; Fontaine et al., 2006[199]; Manning et al., 
2017[197]; Phillipsen et al., 1997[200]; Slot, 2018[201]). The IEA Pre-Primary Project, a 10-country study of 
preschools, showed that the duration of pre-service education was strongly associated with children’s 
higher language scores at age 7 (Montie, Xiang and Schweinhart, 2006[202]). 
More specifically, ECEC staff with a four-year university degree score higher on classroom or playroom 
environment according to the ECERS-R (Early et al., 2007[180])2. In addition, ECEC staff with a four-year 
college degree and a teaching certificate specialised in early childhood education were more likely to 
create higher-quality development, well-being, and learning environments, compared to ECEC staff with 
no formal training in early childhood (Pianta et al., 2005[6]; Sylva et al., 2004a[24]). 
These findings are supported by Hong and colleagues' (2019) analysis of six large-scale US studies, 
which found teacher education to be associated with child outcomes. In some studies, children had even 
showed gains in language and cognition that lasted through to the second year of primary education 
(Whitebook and Ryan, 2011[203]). A study of Peruvian ECEC programmes, conducted by Hanno and 
colleagues (2020[194]), found similar results, with children in classrooms or playrooms facilitated by 
teachers with a university degree attaining higher scores on a vocabulary test (PPVT), when controlling 
for children and setting variables. 
These outcome gains may be at least partly attributed to improvements in staff-child interactions that 
are observed in ECEC practitioners with university-level qualifications. For instance, the National Child 
Care Staffing Study (NCCSS) found that ECEC staff with a four-year degree were more sensitive than 
ECEC staff with two or less years of training (Howes, Whitebook and Phillips, 1992[204]), and children 
with more sensitive and responsive educators tend to achieve better language outcomes and engage 
in higher levels of peer play (Howes, Whitebook and Phillips, 1992[204]; Melhuish et al., 1990[205]). 
Moreover, professional, trained ECEC staff are more likely to engage with children in an age-appropriate 
manner, in comparison to non-professional staff, when it comes to developing socio-emotional and 
verbal skills (Katz, 1983[206]; Shonkoff and Phillips, 2000[207]). Having a credential in early childhood 
education has also been linked to higher overall quality in ECEC settings (Manning et al., 2017[197]; 
Torquati, Raikes and Huddleston-Casas, 2007[198]). 
Meta-analytical evidence supports these findings. Higher teacher qualifications are significantly and 
positively associated with higher-quality ECEC provision (Manning et al., 2019[208]). In addition, Nocita 
and colleagues (2020[209])found a positive association between teachers’ early childhood specialisation 
and children’s language and socioemotional development outcomes; however, this was a weak 
association. 
Hence, the question lies not in the presence or absence of an association of ECEC training programmes 
with the quality of ECEC provision, but in the magnitude of this association. That the meta-analysis by 
Nocita and colleagues (2020[209]) found a weak association may be due to previously discussed 

44    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
variations in content, modality, duration, and quality across the wide range of ECEC programmes, as 
discussed earlier. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
Based on the previously presented evidence, it would be reasonable to expect a lot of variability in the 
characteristics and content of pre-service education and training programmes for ECEC, both between 
and within countries. Similarly, one could also expect to find both highly trained staff and staff with little 
training, between and within countries. Therefore, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 remains interested in 
explore the types and levels of qualifications, as well as the content and duration of initial training 
received by ECEC staff and leaders across countries. 
The indicators and dimensions of staff and leader demographic and educational background can be 
summarised as follows: 
• 
Gender 
• 
Age 
• 
Linguistic background 
• 
Years of experience in ECEC 
• 
Content of prior formal education 
• 
Characteristics of prior formal education 
• 
Qualifications gained from prior formal education 
Cross-content area analyses 
The evidence presented earlier in this sub-section also suggests a relationship between ECEC staff’s 
initial education, the practices they use, and the quality of ECEC provision. As such, TALIS Starting 
Strong will help further develop an understanding of the relationship between initial ECEC education 
and staff practices with children. 
Continuing professional development 
A well-trained and knowledgeable workforce is a critical component of a quality ECEC programme (Slot, 
2018[201]; Zaslow and Martinez-Beck, 2006[210]). Studies have shown positive associations between staff 
professional development and staff-child interactions (Egert, Dederer and Fukkink, 2020[211]; OECD, 
2018b[10]; Slot, 2018[201]). 
Continuing professional development (CPD) (also referred to as ‘continuing professional learning’ 
(Ainley et al., in press[212])) can be understood as activities that seek to strengthen the skills and 
knowledge of currently employed ECEC staff, as well as improve their effectiveness (Neuman and 
Cunningham, 2009[213]). Professional development activities vary widely, ranging from one-hour 
workshops, to multi-year-long coaching programmes, to professional learning communities. They also 
vary widely in terms of content, delivery format, location, and number of participants (Zaslow et al., 
2010[214]). 
CPD provides ECEC staff with the opportunity to critically reflect on their practices, which can in turn 
improve the quality of their interactions with children, families, colleagues, and other stakeholders within 
the ECEC ecosystem. It also helps ECEC staff keep abreast of new developments in the field. Overall, 
studies have shown positive associations between staff professional development and staff-child 

   45 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
interactions (Egert, Dederer and Fukkink, 2020[211]; OECD, 2018b[10]; Slot, 2018[201]). CPD can also be 
a tool to bridge gaps in the knowledge and skills of low-qualified staff (OECD, 2018b[10]). 
The success of CPD depends in part on the supply of effective programmes; a programme can be 
considered effective if it sufficiently addresses the learning needs of ECEC staff. Time and resource 
investments on the part of ECEC settings are also necessary to the success of CPD. Of course,
