participants’ motivation and disposition to learn and apply newly acquired competences are important 
as well. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 aims to help identify the professional development needs of ECEC staff, as 
well as the different incentives and barriers to their participation in professional development activities. 
This information can help countries and jurisdictions put in place the conditions needed for effective 
CPD. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Since the competencies of ECEC staff are commonly acknowledged as determinants of children’s 
outcomes, more and more countries are focusing on CPD (Sheridan et al., 2009[215]; Slot, 2018[201]). As 
noted earlier, CPD opportunities for ECEC staff can take different forms, including but not limited to 
academic courses with degrees or credentials, coaching and mentoring, and professional learning 
communities (Buysse, Winton and Rous, 2009[216]; Egert, Dederer and Fukkink, 2020[211]; Fuligni et al., 
2009[217]). ECEC settings may also run their own internal CPD activities, such as communities of practice 
and induction activities for new staff. 
ECEC staff value professional development and generally perceive it to be useful, since it can inform 
their practice (Gomez and Ford, 2017[218]; Méndez et al., 2017[219]; Moor et al., 2005[220]; Stormont and 
Young-Walker, 2017[221]). Indeed, several studies have found a significant association between ECEC 
staff’s participation in CPD and children’s outcomes, even though it is not always clear how these 
processes function (Ciesielski and Creaghead, 2020[222]; Jensen and Rasmussen, 2019[223]; Sheridan 
et al., 2009[215]; Slot, 2018[201]). A meta-analysis by Egert and colleagues (2018[224]) suggests that 
participation in CPD improves children’s outcomes by way of improving classroom/playroom quality; in 
other words, classroom/playroom quality mediates the relationship between CPD participation and 
children’s outcomes. A systematic review by Brunsek and colleagues (2020[225]) found that CPD 
improved children’s outcomes when the content areas of the professional development activity 
corresponded with the outcome measures. 
Some studies even suggest that, compared to initial ECEC training, in-service training – especially 
training on guided play, collaborative work, and appropriate emergent literacy, mathematics, and 
science activities – has a larger positive effect on staff-child interactions (Assel et al., 2007[226]; Ciesielski 
and Creaghead, 2020[222]; De Haan et al., 2013[227]; Sylva et al., 2007[228]; Zaslow et al., 2010[214]). 
Furthermore, according to one Chilean study, educators believe that their participation in CPD has an 
impact on their practices and that it is critical to their career progression (Gomez and Ford, 2017[218]). In 
addition, ECEC staff in New York stated that professional development was one of the reasons they 
would not leave the profession (Blank et al., 2006[229]). These studies show a potential link between 
continuing professional development and ECEC staff retention. 
Type of continuing professional development 
CPD may consist of formal or informal learning opportunities (Richter et al., 2014[230]). On one hand, 
formal professional learning takes place in structured learning environments, such as mandated staff 
development or graduate coursework. On the other hand, informal professional learning does not follow 
a specified curriculum, is not restricted to a specific learning environment, and is generally voluntary. 

46    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Examples of informal professional learning are unstructured coaching and grassroots communities of 
practice. 
Traditionally, however, formal CPD in ECEC has been delivered in one-off sessions. These forms of 
CPD have been criticised for targeting only short-term changes in ECEC staff practice. In addition, critics 
have claimed that these forms of CPD ignore realities faced by ECEC staff in classrooms, playrooms, 
and ECEC settings (Schachter, Gerde and Hatton-Bowers, 2019[231]). As such, while one-off training 
sessions could certainly form part of a broader CPD toolbox, they should not be the core method in any 
ECEC setting’s professional development strategy. 
Enrolling ECEC staff in higher education coursework may be a suitable alternative. University-based 
CPD can range from short-duration certificate courses to two-, three- or four-year programmes that lead 
to advanced academic degrees. In addition, micro-credentials3, while still an emerging form of 
qualification, could serve as a more flexible, personalised, and competency-based professional 
development pathway for ECEC staff (Hunt et al., 2019[232]). Since micro-credentials are competency-
based, personalised, and taken on-demand, ECEC practitioners can choose to focus on a discrete skill 
that is currently most applicable to their professional needs, their students’, or their setting’s needs (Crow 
and Pipkin, 2017[233]). 
Structured coaching and mentoring schemes are one of the most effective types of CPD in ECEC 
(Brunsek et al., 2020[225]). Coaching is understood as an individualised and ongoing learning process 
where a more experienced practitioner (coach) accompanies another practitioner (coachee), usually 
onsite (Elek and Page, 2019[234]). 
A review of coaching programmes identified four critical features: feedback, observation, goal-setting, 
and reflection (Elek and Page, 2019[234]). Guided by their coach, the coachee takes stock of their 
previous experiences and any new knowledge they have acquired through the coaching process and 
use those to improve their practice (Rogers, Brown and Poblete, 2020[235]). 
Video-coaching can also be a powerful tool for prompting collective reflection and providing pedagogical 
guidance (Balduzzi et al., 2020[236]). The potential benefits of using digital technologies for ECEC staff 
professional development is explored further in a later sub-section. 
These staff coaching programmes can increase classroom/playroom quality and improve staff-child 
interactions (Fiene, 2002[237]). Onsite coaching, combined with intensive curricula, showed promising 
results in improving classroom/playroom quality and child development outcomes (Burchinal et al., 
2008[238]). A meta-analysis of research on teacher coaching confirms these findings: coaching has a 
moderate effect on teaching and a small effect on student achievement (Kraft, Blazar and Hogan, 
2018[239]). 
Participating in CPD alongside groups of teachers and administrators serving children of different ages 
can help foster a community and culture of learning, which encourages embedding new knowledge into 
practice (Zaslow et al., 2010[214]). These types of professional networks – which may be known more 
formally as ‘professional learning communities’ or ‘communities of practice’ – give participants a chance 
to collectively reflect on their daily practices, in their daily context. This form of professional development 
promotes teacher learning (Rogers, 2017[240]; Zaslow, 2014[241]), individual and collective teacher self-
efficacy, and improvements in children’s outcomes (Vangrieken et al., 2017[242]). As such, professional 
learning communities are becoming increasingly present in the ECEC sector across Europe (Jensen 
and Iannone, 2018[243]), as well as in other countries. 
However, a professional development approach that uses a wide variety of methods seems to work 
best. Indeed, Egert and colleagues’ (2020[211]) meta-analysis found that ECEC staff who participated in 
CPD that combined individual learning opportunities, on-site support, coursework, and workshops, 
improved their classroom/playroom interactions in all three CLASS domains (i.e., instructional support, 

   47 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
emotional support, and classroom/playroom organisation). Independent of the format of professional 
development, digital technology can facilitate its delivery (OECD, 2023[244]). 
Participation in continuing professional development 
Opportunities for CPD participation vary across the OECD’s member countries (Bennett et al., 2003[245]; 
Taguma, Litjens and Makowiecki, 2012[246]). In some countries, participation in CPD has become a 
requirement for ECEC staff, although it may be optional for ECEC staff working with younger children 
or for assistant-level ECEC staff (OECD, 2021[5]). For instance, in Poland and Slovenia, CPD is required 
for career progression and to be eligible for salary increases (European Commission, 2014[247]). In, 
Japan pre-primary teachers are required to attend regulated courses and lectures provided by 
universities and other training institutions to be able to renew their accreditation every ten years (OECD, 
2021[5]). 
Nonetheless, several countries report low CPD uptake (OECD, 2012a[173]). This low uptake could be 
caused by several factors, including but not limited to: (a) lack of information about training opportunities; 
(b) limited time-off from regular work; (c) non-reimbursement of CPD expenses; (d) unclear articulation 
of the benefits of CPD, and (e) lack of CPD offerings that correspond with what ECEC staff wish to learn 
(OECD, 2012a[173]). According to TALIS Starting Strong 2018, lack of support from ECEC setting leaders 
and staffing challenges are also common barriers to CPD participation (OECD, 2019c[177]). 
It must also be noted that, while it is common for ECEC staff in settings serving children aged three 
years or older to have opportunities to participate in CPD, this is less the case for settings serving 
younger children. Moreover, a 2019 OECD survey on ECEC policies revealed that incentives for 
assistant-level ECEC staff to participate in professional development activities are not regulated or are 
not required in most countries and jurisdictions (OECD, 2021[5]). 
The results of TALIS Starting Strong 2018 revealed various forms of support that promote ECEC staff 
engagement in CPD. These forms of support include: (a) release from time working with children; (b) 
being provided with materials needed for professional development activities; (c) cost reimbursement; 
(d) monetary incentives; and (e) non-monetary incentives (OECD, 2019c[177]; 2021[5]). 
Results also indicate the importance of involving ECEC staff in identifying CPD needs (OECD, 
2019c[177]). Since lack of alignment between CPD offerings and ECEC staff demand for training is one 
possible cause of low CPD uptake, staff involvement in identifying training needs could potentially 
increase CPD participation. 
ECEC staff in Denmark and Norway reported needing more training to work with children with special 
educational needs, multilingual children, and children from diverse backgrounds (e.g., multicultural, 
economic, religious) (Danmarks Evalueringsinstitut, 2020[169]; Gjerustad, Hjetland and Opheim, 
2019[170]). More generally, training on how to work with children from diverse backgrounds was identified 
as a top professional development need in the results of TALIS Starting Strong 2018 (OECD, 2019c[177]). 
Other studies with ECEC staff in Sweden and Australia indicated a demand for training on how to use 
digital technologies in ECEC (Marklund, 2019[171]; Murcia, Campbell and Aranda, 2018[172]; Otterborn, 
Schönborn and Hultén, 2019[248]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
Given the evidence discussed above, there is likely to be a lot of variability in the content and types of 
activities covered by CPD across different countries in TALIS Starting Strong. Lack of financial support, 
staffing challenges, and disparity between the available CPD offerings and ECEC staff needs are also 
likely to still be common barriers to CPD participation. 

48    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
The indicators and dimensions of professional development can be summarised as follows: 
• 
Participation in continuing professional development activities 
• 
Engagement in collaborative professional practices 
• 
Type and content of continuing professional development 
• 
Incentives and resources to participate in continuing professional development 
• 
Perceived usefulness of continuing professional development 
• 
Barriers to continuing professional development 
• 
Staff needs for further continuing professional development 
• 
Use of digital technologies for continuing professional development. 
Cross-content area analyses 
Given previously discussed evidence of a potential association between various features of professional 
development and ECEC staff practices, this could be an area of analysis for TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
It would also be expected that participation in professional development could partly offset ECEC staff’s 
lack of practical experience. The relationship between various features of pre-service training and ECEC 
staff’s professional development needs can also be explored. 
Well-being 
The COVID-19 pandemic has brought the physical and mental health of educational practitioners to the 
fore (Eadie et al., 2021[249]). However, ECEC staff well-being has always had huge policy relevance, 
especially when considering how ECEC staff well-being influences the quality of ECEC provision. After 
all, research shows that ECEC staff well-being is associated with the quality of staff-child interactions 
(De Schipper et al., 2008[250]; Grant, Jeon and Buettner, 2019[251]; Jennings, Jeon and Roberts, 2020[252]; 
La Paro et al., 2009[138]; Rimm-Kaufman and Hamre, 2010[253]). ECEC staff well-being has also been 
found to be associated with children’s well-being (OECD, 2020g[254]). 
The association between the well-being of ECEC staff and their intention to stay in the profession further 
reinforces the policy relevance of ECEC staff well-being. High levels of staff turnover is endemic to the 
ECEC sector, with most of the OECD’s member countries seeing low retention rates among their ECEC 
staff (Fenech, Sumsion and Goodfellow, 2006[255]; Huntsman, 2008[256]). Hence, understanding the 
determinants of ECEC staff well-being could help decrease turnover rates (Cormick, McMullen and Lee, 
2021[257]; Kwon et al., 2020[258]; McMullen et al., 2020[259]). 
Working conditions also form an important part of the picture. In fact, Corr et al. (2014[260]) found that 
poor mental health among ECEC staff was linked with poor working conditions. Furthermore, in the US, 
the most common reasons for ECEC staff attrition all pertain to working conditions: inadequate 
administrative support, low compensation, lack of benefits, and negative perceptions about the work 
environment (Porter, 2012[261]). 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 explores ECEC staff well-being from the lens of job satisfaction, stress, 
reasons for staying in the profession, and whether staff feel valued by others. This information can help 
countries and jurisdictions to create conditions that promote the well-being of ECEC staff, which could 
in turn reduce turnover and improve the quality of ECEC provision. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
ECEC staff and leader well-being is understood in this document as a composite of psychological, 
physiological, and occupational well-being (Cumming and Wong, 2019[262]; Kwon et al., 2020[258]; Wong 
et al., 2023[263]). It is a multidimensional construct that involves the interaction of individual, relational 

   49 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
and contextual aspects (Cumming and Wong, 2019[262]; Kwon et al., 2021[264]; Wong et al., 2023[263]). 
Well-being is also related to other content areas of TALIS Starting Strong, such as stakeholder relations, 
organisational climate, and working conditions. 
It must be noted that the relationship between these factors is not only multidimensional but also 
multidirectional. For instance, while there is well-established evidence of external factors influencing 
staff well-being (Cumming and Wong, 2019[262]; Kwon et al., 2021[264]; Wong et al., 2023[263]), Kwon and 
colleagues (Kwon et al., 2021[264]) found that staff’s psychological well-being mediated the relationship 
between working conditions and professional well-being, as well as the relationship between physical 
well-being and professional well-being. In other words, different aspects of staff well-being – 
independent of external factors – also exert an influence on each other. 
Job satisfaction, which refers to the extent to which a person dislikes or likes their job (Cumming and 
Wong, 2019[262]), is often used to operationalise staff well-being (Hall-Kenyon et al., 2014[265]). It has 
been found to be associated with various aspects of the ECEC setting, such as working conditions, 
stress, and relationships with colleagues (Hall-Kenyon et al., 2014[265]; Cumming, 2017[66]; OECD, 
2020b[99]). 
Indeed, good working conditions have the power to attract and retain highly qualified and motivated 
workers. For example, higher wages and better working conditions affect job satisfaction, work 
motivation and, indirectly, the quality of teaching, caring, and interactions with children (Heilala et al., 
2021[266]; Huntsman, 2008[256]; Moon and Burbank, 2004[267]). Establishing fair working conditions – such 
as appropriate pay (‘living wage’) and a supportive organisational climate – promote staff well-being, 
which in turn lead to improvements in children’s development, well-being, and learning outcomes 
(OECD, 2006[268]). Research also suggests that the staff-child ratio in target groups can impact staff job 
satisfaction. Lower staff-child ratios – that is, a smaller number of children per staff member – are 
associated with job satisfaction, since they enable staff to provide better quality care (Munton et al., 
2002[269]). 
On the other hand, low staff compensation has been linked to low morale, weaker career commitment, 
and poorer teaching quality (Barnett, 2003[270]). Furthermore, it has been shown that poor working 
conditions within the ECEC sector – such as low wages, long working hours, and the length of the work 
year in child-care settings (compared to preschools and kindergartens) – deter qualified professionals 
from entering or staying in the profession (Torquati, Raikes and Huddleston-Casas, 2007[198]). 
Relational and contextual factors are also important for understanding job satisfaction (Cumming, 
2017[66]; Hall-Kenyon et al., 2014[265]). For example, according to Jorde-Bloom (1988[271])and Schaack 
and colleagues (2020[272]), organisational commitment and a shared vision are both related to job 
satisfaction. Goelman and colleagues (2006[273]) note that the number of staff within a classroom or 
playroom also impacts job satisfaction. When staff work together in a classroom/playroom, there are 
opportunities for supervision, consultation, and discussing work challenges, which contributes to job 
satisfaction. Autonomy in the workplace is another factor that has a positive effect on job satisfaction 
(Child Care Human Resources Sector Council, 2009[274]). Recent findings also reveal a strong 
association between teacher autonomy and intention to stay in the profession (Worth and Van den 
Brande, 2020[275]). 
Conversely, high levels of work stress for ECEC staff can lead to job dissatisfaction and poorer 
performance. A US survey of teachers (including staff at kindergarten level) found that stress levels had 
increased and job satisfaction had dropped by nearly 25 percentage points during a five-year period 
(Macia, Markow and Lee, 2013[276]). Work stressors are also negatively correlated with teachers’ well-
being (Gu, Wang and You, 2020[277]). In addition, teaching-related stress negatively affects the quality 
of ECEC staff’s interactions with children, as well as their enforcement of rules and routines in the 
classroom or playroom (Penttinen et al., 2020[278]). 

50    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Work stress can also affect turnover rates. Teachers who reported high levels of stress tended to have 
the lowest level of occupational commitment and intention to stay in teaching (Lambert et al., 2019[279]). 
Hence, there is a need to look more closely at the experience of work stress among staff and leaders, 
as well as any differences within countries (Gjerustad et al., 2020[280]; Viernickel, Voss and Mauz, 
2017[281]). 
There are several potential sources of stress for ECEC staff. For instance, staff might feel stressed due 
to the pressures of complying with a top-down curriculum that expects staff to teach children 
developmentally inappropriate academic content. Lack of coping strategies to handle children’s 
challenging behaviours may also result in stress. Insufficient human or financial resources in the setting 
are also a major source for stress for staff, as reported by TALIS Starting Strong 2018 (OECD, 2020b[99]). 
The extent to which staff feel valued by others – such as parents and guardians, children and society – 
also influences their well-being (OECD, 2020b[99]), and thus their intentions to stay in the profession. 
Satisfaction with ECEC as a profession is related to the social value of the profession, which is complex 
and based on numerous factors. In TALIS 2008, intrinsic and extrinsic value, and personal and social 
utility, were found to influence motivations for choosing teaching as a career; social factors, such as the 
esteem in which the profession is held, also influenced this choice. 
The balance of these factors can vary between countries. For example, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 
results showed cross-country variations in the extent to which ECEC staff feel valued: in Israel, 75% of 
pre-primary staff ‘strongly agreed’ that ECEC staff were valued by society, whereas in Japan, only 31% 
felt similarly (OECD, 2020b[99]). In the US, ECEC staff who felt their work allowed them to make a positive 
difference in children’s lives were more likely to stay in their position and not change professions (Gable 
and Hunting, 2001[282]). On the other hand, interviews with ECEC staff and recent graduates in Ireland 
indicated that they do not intend to work in the ECEC field indefinitely, and that they envisioned the 
possibility of converting to primary school teaching or other employment where they felt their work would 
be better recognised and valued (Moloney, 2010[283]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
Based on the research described above, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 has the potential to further 
examine the relationship between job satisfaction and sources of work stress. It would also be possible 
to examine the association between job satisfaction and perceived social value of the profession. The 
incorporation of ECEC staff well-being in TALIS Starting Strong 2024 also provides opportunities to 
address research questions concerned with the following factors: 
• 
Within and between-country heterogeneity of satisfaction with ECEC as a profession 
• 
Within and between-country differences in workplace stress 
• 
Within and between-country variations of perception of societal value of the profession 
Hence, indicators and dimensions concerned with ECEC well-being can be summarised as follows. 
• 
Satisfaction with career and profession 
• 
Satisfaction with levels of autonomy afforded 
• 
Sources of work stress 
• 
Perception of societal value of the profession 

   51 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Cross-content area analyses 
It would be expected that there would be positive correlations between staff well-being (e.g., satisfaction 
with working environment) and pedagogical practices and process quality in ECEC (De Schipper et al., 
2008[250]), as well as associations with administrative/pedagogical leadership (Porter, 2012[261]). 
ECEC setting characteristics 
Structural quality 
Structural quality refers to characteristics which are thought to indirectly influence child development 
and are considered to be important preconditions of process quality (Slot, Lerkkanen and Leseman, 
2015[284]). Structural quality characteristics ensure that children are in settings that meet basic health 
and safety requirements, while also providing the enabling conditions for children to flourish. 
There is no one unique list of structural quality features, but it is possible to organise them into three 
levels (Early et al., 2007[180]; Halle et al., 2011[285]; Kuger et al., 2016[286]; Slot, 2018[201]): 
i. 
Setting-level (e.g., physical environment, location, size, staff turnover, financial resources, 
and health, safety, and nutrition); 
ii. 
Classroom/playroom-level (e.g., staff-to-child ratio, group size, group composition); and 
iii. 
Staff-level (e.g., staff characteristics, working conditions, qualifications, and professional 
development). 
A common feature of structural characteristics is that they are measurable, and therefore they can be 
more readily regulated. Many countries prescribe minimum structural conditions for ECEC settings to 
be able to operate, receive public funding, or be certified as educational programmes. Setting these 
minimum standards allows countries to ensure safety, homogenise services, and offer all children similar 
quality conditions, contributing to a more equitable ECEC system (OECD, 2015a[287]). 
These structural characteristics are necessary conditions to ensure process quality, but not sufficient on 
their own. However, it is important to note that the effects of individual structural characteristics are 
difficult to isolate, given how children develop and learn in a context where staff, setting, and 
classroom/playroom-level characteristics are intertwined (Slot, 2018[201]). 
Indeed, elements of structural quality tend to be interdependent, and this interdependence can create 
inequities. For instance, settings with higher staff-to-child ratios might offer better overall working 
conditions and be able to employ more highly qualified ECEC staff; the converse also holds, since 
settings with poorer working conditions struggle to attract qualified ECEC staff. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
For process quality and learning to occur, as well as to support children’s development, well-being, and 
learning – there are minimum structural conditions that need to be met, which is why it is relevant to 
capture information on structural characteristics. However, it is important to note that defining acceptable 
minimum thresholds is more straightforward for certain structural characteristics than others. For 
instance, while hygiene and safety minimums for an ECEC setting are clear-cut, the acceptable 
minimum for the ratio of qualified teachers per child or the maximum number of children in the 
classroom/playroom is not as easy to determine. 
 Regarding setting-level characteristics, physical spaces – both indoor and outdoor – need to be large 
enough for children to move safely and freely, be well-ventilated, and have low levels of noise. 
Equipment and furniture also need to be in good shape (Banghart et al., 2020[288]). Furthermore, children 

52    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
and adults need to feel safe in their learning environment and secure in the fact that their basic needs 
are being met (Cleveland, 2018[289]). Water, sanitation, and hygiene are also minimum conditions for the 
learning process (Arshad, Qamar and Gulzar, 2018[290]; Coronel Ugaz, 2017[291]). The COVID-19 
pandemic has demonstrated how these basic conditions are fundamental to avoid disease transmission 
while children attend educational institutions. 
The location of ECEC settings is also relevant to the quality of ECEC provision; it can influence children’s 
transitions and equitable access to quality ECEC. Geographical location (i.e., urban vs rural) is linked 
to differences in the quality of ECEC provision, and this also seems to interact with children’s age. In 
addition, higher quality staff-child relationships were observed in ECEC settings that were located inside 
schools for children aged 3 to 6, compared to ECEC settings outside school grounds or independent 
settings (Slot et al., 2017[55]). 
Researchers explain these findings by suggesting that staff-child quality interactions have different 
characteristics across different age groups (OECD, 2018b[10]). For example, scarce or low-quality 
resources – which is a challenge that rural settings are more frequently faced with - might have a less 
pronounced negative effect on outcomes for children under the age of three, where greater emphasis 
tends to be placed instead on informal conversations and routine care. Furthermore, ecological 
conceptualisations of early child development postulate that ECEC setting structural quality may be 
mediated or moderated by the social, economic, and cultural characteristics of the neighbourhood and 
its built environment (Minh et al., 2017[292]). 
The type (public, private, mixed) or amount of funding ECEC settings receive is also associated with 
programme quality. Funding may impact relationships within the setting, working conditions, and 
resources (OECD, 2018a[293]). For instance, the availability of funding influences a setting’s ability to 
access and use digital technologies or secure technical support (Burns and Gottschalk, 2020[294]; 
Schleicher, 2019[183]; Zabatiero et al., 2018[295]). 
Staff turnover is another determinant of the quality of ECEC provision (Phillips, Austin and Whitebook, 
2016[296]). High turnover has been found to be associated with a lower quality of staff-child interactions, 
which can then impede children’s development, well-being, and learning outcomes (Jennings and 
Greenberg, 2009[297]; Totenhagen et al., 2016[298]; Melhuish and Gardiner, 2019[299]). Staff turnover also 
increases the likelihood of the remaining staff leaving, thus exacerbating staffing issues (Whitebook and 
Sakai, 2003[300]). In sum, high levels of staff turnover has far-reaching effects on ECEC quality, affecting 
process quality, children’s development, learning and well-being, and the staff remaining in the setting 
(Cassidy et al., 2011[301]). 
Structural quality characteristics at the classroom or playroom-level seem to be a more proximal 
predictor of process quality than setting-level characteristics (OECD, 2018b[10]; Slot, 2018[201]). 
Specifically, smaller group sizes and higher staff-child ratios are thought to enable staff to have higher-
quality interactions with each child, develop a better knowledge of the child’s needs, and facilitate 
activities tailored to each child’s needs (Bowne et al., 2017[302]), as well as respond to children’s needs 
in a timely manner (Banghart et al., 2020[288]). 
Features related to group size and staff-child ratio are cited as consistent predictors of 
classroom/playroom quality in various OECD countries. Smaller group sizes and higher staff-child ratios 
have been linked to improvements in staff-child interactions and higher benefits for children under the 
age of three (CEPI, 2017[303]; OECD, 2006[268]; Sabol et al., 2013[304]; Sim et al., 2019[22]; Ulferts, Wolf 
and Anders, 2019[75]). The quality of staff-child interactions has also been found to be higher in settings 
where the group size was of the recommended size or below (Burchinal, Howes and Kontos, 2002[305]; 
Huntsman, 2008[256]; NICHD Early Child Care Research Network, 2000[306]) as ECEC staff acted in a 
more caring way and stimulated action and thinking more often. Conversely, where group sizes were 
larger than recommended, process quality was poorer (Burchinal et al., 2000[307]). 

   53 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
For instance, children in Mexican preschools with higher staff-child ratios scored higher in cognitive 
development tests than those in preschools with lower staff-child ratios. Schools with higher ratios also 
had better-trained teachers, better management, and multiple classrooms/playrooms (Myers, 2004[308]). 
On the other hand, ECEC staff in Mexico who worked in preschools where the staff-child ratio 
reached 1:30 expressed an inability to provide individualised attention to the children under their care 
(Yoshikawa et al., 2007[309]). 
Similarly, secondary analyses of five European datasets (from England, the Netherlands, Finland, 
Portugal and Germany) conducted by Slot, Lerkkanen, and Leseman (2015[284]). found further evidence 
that group size and staff-child ratio were related to higher levels of observed process quality. However, 
these analyses also suggested that the association between the said structural quality features and 
process quality was not straightforward. For instance, in Finland, group size was associated with the 
organisation of the ECEC setting: in preschools located in school settings, larger groups were 
associated with higher process quality, whereas the opposite was true in community-based settings 
(Slot, Lerkkanen and Leseman, 2015[284]). 
 However, while most studies confirm the relationship between group size and staff-child ratio and 
process quality, some studies have found null effects for staff-child ratio and even a negative effect of 
small group size (Slot, 2018[201]). In this regard, it might be interesting to explore if there is a threshold 
for an optimal group size or staff-child ratio that enables high-quality pedagogical practices (as reported 
by staff), and how such a threshold might interact with other staff- and setting-level factors across 
countries and jurisdictions. Indeed, the relationship between group size, staff-child ratios, and child 
outcomes is complex. For instance, a meta-analysis of US studies of preschool children found nonlinear 
associations between staff-child ratios and child scores in cognitive and achievement tests (Bowne 
et al., 2017[310]). 
Staff-level characteristics that comprise process quality (staff characteristics, initial education, 
continuing professional development, and working conditions) are also critical for process quality 
(OECD, 2018b[10]). TALIS Starting Strong 2024 faces the challenge of capturing staff-child ratio in the 
most consistent way between and within countries and jurisdictions, as it is not always a stable variable 
(might change during the day, within days, etc). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
Given the evidence relating structural characteristics to process quality and child development outcomes 
[e.g., OECD (2006[268]); Burchinal, Howes, et al. (2008[238]); Barros and Aguiar (2010[311]); Morrissey 
(2010[312]); Sabol et al. (2013[304])], TALIS Starting Strong 2024 provides an opportunity to explore the 
indicators at the three levels: setting, classroom/playroom, and staff. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 includes measures of multiple structural quality characteristics: 
• 
Setting location and environment of the neighbourhood 
• 
Setting funding and budget constraints 
• 
Resources, including digital technologies 
• 
Physical space 
• 
Setting staff human resources 
• 
Staff turnover 
• 
Staff-child ratio and group size. 
Structural conditions of ECEC settings can vary substantially across countries (OECD, 2015a[287]; Pardo 
and Adlerstein, 2016[174]). It is important to examine what drives this variability. This would elucidate how 

54    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
different structural features in ECEC settings across different countries and jurisdictions may bundle 
together or be associated with process quality. 
Cross-content area analyses 
The previously listed indicators are relevant both as a predictor and an outcome in analyses. The 
manner by which structural quality characteristics can predict different outcomes has already been 
discussed in other parts of this document. However, it is also important to note that structural quality 
characteristics are themselves influenced by other variables, such as ECEC staff preferences (e.g., do 
better educated staff work in settings with smaller groups and lower child-staff ratio?). 
Moreover, given how ecological conceptualisations of ECEC postulate that children’s outcomes may be 
mediated or moderated by neighbourhood characteristics (Minh et al., 2017[292]), TALIS Starting Strong 
2024 provides insight into various neighbourhood-level characteristics. Neighbourhood-level 
characteristics are an external context, which, in conjunction with setting-level contextual factors (such 
as child and staff characteristics, as well as setting resources) can inform analysis. Finally, since staff 
turnover is a concern in many countries, understanding its associations with various work-related factors 
(including professional development, well-being, and resources) will be highly relevant. 
Pedagogical and administrative leadership 
TALIS Starting Strong considers the ECEC leader to be the person with the most responsibility for the 
administrative, managerial or pedagogical leadership at the ECEC setting. The leader plays a crucial 
role in various aspects of ECEC delivery within settings, including but not limited to curriculum, 
pedagogy, in-service training of staff, and teamwork. Leadership can also extend to aspects of external 
supports and services (e.g., education, health, and community services). 
Facilitating teamwork is critical to high-quality ECEC provision, and the ECEC leader creates the 
atmosphere for this. Poor leadership can undermine teamwork by creating competition, resentment, and 
lack of respect among staff. Autonomy and authority to make instructional and pedagogical decisions 
are also important aspects of teachers’ work (Ingersoll and Collins, 2019[313]). In turn, the autonomy and 
authority that teachers have over instructional and pedagogical decisions depend, at least in part, on 
how school leaders organise work in their educational setting (Wenner and Campbell, 2017[314]). This 
also applies to the ECEC context (Havnes, 2018[315]). 
Leadership is a complex undertaking (Sims, Waniganayake and Hadley, 2017[316]; Sisson et al., 
2021[317]). Since leaders are involved in a multitude of everyday activities within an ECEC setting, the 
distinction between administrative and pedagogical leadership has received increased interest (Bøe and 
Hognestad, 2018[318]). 
In sum, leadership is key to organisational learning, knowledge development, and staff motivation; the 
leader is responsible for creating a stimulating environment that supports children’s early development, 
well-being, and learning (Vannebo and Gotvassli, 2014[319]). It is therefore important to collect data on 
what ECEC setting leadership entails, from the perspective of both leaders and staff. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Research on leadership in ECEC has increased dramatically since the development of the first cycle of 
TALIS Starting Strong [e.g. Pascal et al. (2018[320]); Granrusten et al. (2018[321]); Aubrey (2019[322]); 
Strehmel et al. (2019[323]); Nicholson et al. (2020[324]); Karaoğlu (2020[325]); Heikkinen et al. (2021[326]); 
Movahedazarhouligh (2021[327])]. Through this research, effective leadership has been identified as a 
determinant of quality in ECEC settings (Bloom and Bella, 2005[328]; Gray, 2004[329]). For instance, 
the Effective Leadership in Early Years (ELEY) study revealed that effective ECEC leadership positively 
affects children’s well-being, as well as their educational, health, and social achievements (Siraj-

   55 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Blatchford and Manni, 2007[330]). However, there appear to be cross-country disparities in research 
activity in this field. In a review of research on this topic, Fonsén and colleagues (2019[331]) found that 
Finland and the United States have extensive research on ECEC leadership compared to Germany, 
Singapore, South Africa and Japan where research into ECEC leadership is less developed. 
The role of school leaders in improving teaching and learning is increasingly recognised; this is known 
as ‘pedagogical leadership’ (Siraj and Hallet, 2013[332]). Pedagogical leadership is focused on the need 
to develop skills in leading organisational change in early childhood settings (Andrews, 2009[333]). Ideally, 
pedagogical leadership should form a bridge between research and practice by disseminating new 
information and shaping agendas (Kagan and Hallmark, 2001[334]). This approach to leadership is based 
on a passion for learning, and is different from instructional leadership, which relates to the transmission 
of knowledge rather than the construction, co-construction, or creation of knowledge (Siraj and Hallet, 
2013[332]). Among other capabilities, the leaders’ ability to reason, problem-solve, evaluate, give 
constructive feedback, and learn from and with others, is important for their pedagogical leadership 
(Hallet, 2012[335]). 
A study in Finland specified four dimensions that influence the success of pedagogical leadership: 
i) context; ii) organisational culture; iii) leader’s professionalism; and iv) management of substance 
(Fonsén, 2013[336]). In addition, four factors were identified as facilitating the performance of pedagogical 
leadership responsibilities: i) adequate resources (e.g., sufficient personnel, ample time to work, 
reasonable workload); ii) personnel management skills; iii) pedagogical management skills (including 
knowledge of pedagogy and recent developments in the ECEC sector); and iv) confidence in 
organisation’s senior staff level (including their supervisors, other management, and administration). 
However, the role of an ECEC leader is not limited to pedagogical leadership. Administrative leadership 
is an equally important aspect of ECEC leadership. There is growing demand for strong leadership, 
given the need for increased accountability, competition, and financial constraints within the ECEC 
sector (Muijs et al., 2004[337]). Amidst these financial constraints, the quality and pedagogy of ECEC 
may be affected by how financial resources are spent – that is, if resources are invested into staff 
professional development, staff recruitment, staff salaries, or buildings and equipment (Wall, Taguma 
and Litjens, 2015[338]). 
Fostering aspiring leaders is also important; Rodd (2013[339]) points out that formal leaders who do not 
allow others to aspire to leadership are failing in their responsibility to build leadership capacity and plan 
for succession. As demonstrated by one study in the United States, the relationship between ECEC staff 
and the setting leader greatly influenced staff motivation for professional growth (Wagner and French, 
2010[340]). 
Indeed, good management practices are important for increasing staff satisfaction and the quality of 
ECEC provision (Aubrey, Godfrey and Harris, 2013[341]). Nonetheless, despite the equal importance of 
administrative leadership, some studies have found that ECEC leaders felt less prepared for 
administrative responsibilities (such as financial management); they felt better prepared for 
responsibilities related to being ECEC educators and building relationships with staff (Hayden, 1997[342]; 
Muijs et al., 2004[337]). 
Furthermore, many ECEC staff are unwilling to take on leadership roles for various reasons (Rodd, 
2006[343]), including but not limited to poor working conditions, low pay, low status, lack of understanding 
of employment rights, and the stressful and physically demanding nature of the work itself. 
Understanding the working conditions of leaders can contribute to improving the quality of ECEC 
(OECD, 2012b[344]). 
Increasing diversity in both the ECEC workforce and in the population of children in ECEC settings is a 
growing consideration for ECEC leaders. As such, the leaders’ role could also include to shift to a 
multicultural/multilinguistic approach by fostering respectful relationships among staff, adopting a 

56    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
collaborative style of leadership, setting clear non-negotiable values, and managing conflicts 
strategically (Derman-Sparks, LeeKeenan and Nimmo, 2015[345]). 
Approaches to leadership are shifting. The traditional leadership model that is mostly hierarchical – with 
a leader who manages, plans, and directs other staff (Rodd, 2013[339]) – is replaced by distributed and 
collaborative models (Starratt, 2003[346]; Starratt, 2003[346]; Spillane, 2005[347]; Duffy and Marshall, 
2007[348]; Fitzgerald and Gunter, 2008[349]; McDowall Clark and Murray, 2012[350]; Rodd, 2013[339]). 
McDowall Clark and Murray (2012[350]) describe this leadership approach – that is, distributed leadership 
– as flexible and non-hierarchical, with members at different levels of the organisation being allowed 
and encouraged to take on leadership responsibilities and to act on challenges and opportunities. 
Leadership can come from various actors within the organisation (Raelin, 2003[351]). 
Distributed leadership is characterised by the following ways of working (Spillane, 2005[347]): 
• 
It involves multiple actors within the setting, including those without formal leadership 
positions. 
• 
It is interconnected, and non-hierarchical working is encouraged. 
• 
Interaction among individuals is the key factor of leadership practice, not only the actions of 
individuals. 
In addition, Fu (2022[352]) lays out four features of distributed pedagogical leadership: (i) shared 
awareness and authority; (ii) emotional competence; (iii) collaboration skills; and (iv) communication 
skills. 
There is emerging observational evidence of these distributed forms of leadership promoting high-
quality pedagogy in ECEC settings (Denee and Thornton, 2021[353]; Fu, 2022[352]; Bratsch-Hines et al., 
2023[354]; Fonsén et al., 2023[355]). In fact, some research even suggests that pedagogical leadership 
can become ineffective when a leader works alone, highlighting the importance of implementing 
distributed leadership (Heikka and Waniganayake, 2011[356]; Heikka, Waniganayake and Hujala, 
2013[357]; Heikka, 2014[358]). 
Nonetheless, Gronn (2008[359]; 2011[360]) has laid out some limitations of distributed leadership. Given 
these limitations, Bøe and Hognestad (2017[361]) have suggested hybrid leadership as an alternative. In 
hybrid leadership, both traditional and distributed forms of leadership can figure in ECEC (Bøe and 
Hognestad, 2017[361]). They emphasise a need to study the intersection of diverse leadership levels and 
structures. Sisson and colleagues (2021[317]) have also proposed another alternative type of leadership 
in ECEC, in the form of co-constructed leadership that goes beyond traditional notions of leadership 
(transformational; transformative; distributed). Instead of promoting a specific early childhood model, 
they formulate a number of principles of co-constructed leadership based on the entangled connections 
between listening, dialogue, and agency. 
However, it is worth noting that what constitutes effective leadership may vary depending on the cultural 
context. For instance, in a review of Chinese ECEC research, Bullough and Palaiologou (2020[362]) 
discuss the cultural embeddedness of leadership, pointing out that school leaders in China exercise 
relatively higher degrees of authority, given societal norms regarding leadership; this poses difficulties 
for pre-school leaders in adopting distributed forms of leadership within their settings and in performing 
leadership responsibilities outside their classroom (p. 58). 
The profile of ECEC leaders (in terms of their education, experience, training, and professional 
development) is also important when discussing effective leadership (as discussed earlier). Recent 
studies have found an unambiguous connection between leader characteristics and ECEC setting 
quality, as operationalised by CLASS and ECERS (Perlman et al., 2020[363]). Notwithstanding, ECEC 
leaders often take up leadership roles without specific training (Aubrey, 2011[364]). For example, in 
Germany, less than half of centre leaders have been trained for pedagogy-specific leadership tasks 

   57 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
(Bader et al., 2019[365]). Moreover, recent research notes the absence of leadership in ECEC training 
curricula (Merenda, 2019[366]). 
Since research suggests that the quality of ECEC services is strongly related to their leaders’ level of 
education and development, attracting well-trained leaders becomes a key issue in ECEC provision 
(OECD, 2012b[344]). However, as noted by Douglass (2019[367]), there have been no large-scale studies 
regarding the characteristics of ECEC leaders and the features of different leadership structures in 
ECEC. This makes it difficult to map out the current strengths and development needs of ECEC leaders. 
As such, Douglass (2019[367]) has highlighted a need to produce more research in the following areas: 
leadership recruitment; preparation and credentials; professional development; compensation and job 
design; and contextual supports needed by leaders (Douglass, 2019[367]). TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
can help generate this information. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
The nature of the leadership responsibilities (i.e., administrative and pedagogical) and approaches used 
(i.e., hierarchical, distributed, hybrid, or others) across ECEC settings can be examined through the 
survey. It would also be possible to explore the relationship between leaders’ beliefs regarding their role 
and their reported leadership practices. In addition, questions regarding leadership issues may be 
presented to both ECEC leaders and staff; this would give a fuller understanding of leadership in the 
ECEC setting. Furthermore, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 provides an opportunity to explore differences 
in leadership structures and approaches across countries. 
Cross-content area analyses 
The evidence discussed previously suggests a link between leadership and the quality of ECEC 
provision. TALIS Starting Strong can explore this link, for instance, by analysing how aspects of 
pedagogical and managerial leadership may be related to various aspects of structural quality and 
process quality in ECEC. 
Evidence also indicates an association between leaders’ characteristics (e.g., their education, 
experience, training, and professional development) and abilities and how leaders perform their 
responsibilities (e.g., management of working condition, resource management, interaction with their 
followers, giving appraisal and feedback etc.). TALIS Starting Strong can also examine these 
associations. 
The list below enumerates the indicators and dimensions concerned with pedagogical and 
administrative leadership: 
• 
Time spent on pedagogical and administrative leadership 
• 
Beliefs about pedagogical, administrative and collaborative leadership 
• 
Education background on leadership 
• 
Professional development needs on leadership 
• 
Providing feedback to staff 
• 
Budget constraints to leadership 
• 
External inspection and monitoring 
• 
Setting resources 
• 
Regulation constraints 
• 
Resources for professional development for staff. 

58    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Organisational climate and working conditions 
Organisational climate refers to staff perceptions of the organisational culture (norms and values, beliefs, 
attitudes and practices) which distinguishes their organization from other comparable institutions. When 
collective perceptions, attitudes, beliefs, and values of individuals that are in one way or another involved 
in the organisational processes of an ECEC setting are combined with the personalities that come 
together and the leadership that guides them, an organisational climate emerges (Bloom, 2010[368]). 
Since ECEC setting leaders play a critical role in shaping the attitudes, beliefs, motivations, and 
relationships of their staff (Strehmel et al., 2019[323]), leadership is thus an important determinant of 
organisational climate. 
The environmental and relational factors described above – which, together, comprise the organisational 
climate – influence ECEC staff’s psychological and emotional well-being (Cumming, 2017[66]). Given the 
well-established association between staff well-being and process quality (De Schipper et al., 2008[250]; 
La Paro et al., 2009[138]; Rimm-Kaufman and Hamre, 2010[253]; Grant, Jeon and Buettner, 2019[251]; 
Jennings, Jeon and Roberts, 2020[252]), organisational climate therefore becomes an important 
contributor to process quality. Indeed, evidence from the Researching Effective Pedagogy in Early Years 
(REPEY) study in England suggests that effective practice needs an appropriate working climate, 
assessment, management, staff development and support for staff work, as well as pedagogical 
understanding about classroom or playroom activities (OECD, 2010[369]). These factors are also thought 
to be related to effective leadership, especially that which promotes a team culture in which all members 
are valued and respected within a climate of trust (Jones and Pound, 2008[370]). 
Working conditions constitute a particular dimension of organisational climate and pertain to a set of 
structural characteristics that can influence the motivation and satisfaction of ECEC staff with their 
chosen profession. These characteristics include but are not limited to: pay; the organisation of work 
and work activities; training, skills, and employability; health, safety and well-being; and working time 
and work-life balance. Since working conditions affect ECEC staff in different ways, working conditions 
therefore become are related to ECEC setting quality (Lower and Cassidy, 2007[371]; Dennis and 
O’Connor, 2013[372]; Logan, Cumming and Wong, 2020[373]; Benchekroun, Cameron and Marmot, 
2020[374]) and, in turn, to children’s development, well-being, and learning outcomes. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
The close relationship between organisational climate, job satisfaction, role stress, and burnout is well-
documented (Pecino et al., 2019[375]). Moreover, job resources, such as autonomy, skill variety, 
performance feedback, and opportunities for growth, can have a buffering role on the impact of various 
job demands on burnout (Bakker and Demerouti, 2017[376]). An organisational climate characterised by 
collaboration, trust, and open communication has also been found to positively correlate with a higher 
quality of staff-child interactions (Bennett et al., 2003[245]; Sylva et al., 2004b[377]; Bloom and Bella, 
2005[328]; Veziroglu-Celik and Yildiz, 2018[378]; Kang, 2020[379]). In fact, a number of studies have found 
that, compared to staff-child ratios, organisational climate has an even stronger association with the 
quality of ECEC provision (OECD, 2018b[10]). For these reasons, various aspects of organisational 
climate have been considered to be important determinants of the quality of ECEC provision (OECD, 
2017a[380]). 
Working conditions represent one aspect of organisational climate. Factors like workload and working 
hours, salary, contract type, career progression, and management characteristics influence the 
motivation and satisfaction of ECEC staff. Better working conditions, such as competitive salaries and 
good working hours, help retain effective ECEC staff, as well as attract young people to the workforce; 
these can indirectly influence process quality (Moon and Burbank, 2004[267]; Fenech, Sumsion and 
Goodfellow, 2006[255]; Huntsman, 2008[256]). 

   59 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
On the other hand, poor working conditions and poor compensation can lead to high turnover rates in 
the sector, which disrupts continuity of care, professional development efforts, harms overall quality and 
negatively affects child outcomes (Siraj-Blatchford et al., 2002[381]; Elliott, 2006[382]; Phillips, Austin and 
Whitebook, 2016[296]). Retention and contract type/employment status are also expected to be 
determinants of quality in ECEC provision, although a recent study in Ecuador found that contract status 
did not predict quality in kindergarten classes (Araujo et al., 2016[383]). Notwithstanding, many countries’ 
ECEC policy continues to pay little attention to the quality of work environments in ECEC settings [see, 
for example: (Cumming, Wong and Logan, 2021[384])]. 
ECEC staff working hours and how staff spend their working time are both important working conditions 
as well. Surveys and high-frequency observations within ECEC settings can provide rich detail regarding 
how ECEC staff and children spend their time. Typical activity categories for how time is spent in ECEC 
settings include but are not limited to: (a) activities with children; (b) individual planning for preparing 
activities; (c) teamwork and dialogue with colleagues; (d) participating in ECEC management; (e) 
general administrative communication and paperwork; (f) communicating with parents; (g) engaging in 
extracurricular activities; and (h) professional development activities. However, research investigating 
how ECEC practitioners use their time is still relatively nascent (De Haan, Elbers and Leseman, 
2014[385]), especially in comparison to time use research with primary and secondary school teachers 
(TNS BMRB, 2014[386]). 
Nonetheless, some organisations have recently collected comparable data on ECEC practitioners’ time 
use and overall working time. For example, the International Labour Organization (ILO) collected reports 
on contact time and overall time. The overall distribution of time and contact time in six OECD countries 
indicated that hours for ECEC staff were relatively long, compared to hours for primary school 
teachers (ILO, 2012[387]). ECEC staff’s contact hours were also long, leaving little remaining time for 
preparation, professional development, consultation with parents, or other supporting activities (ILO, 
2012[387]). 
An OECD (2017c[388]) survey across 19 countries reported similar results, with 11 countries reporting 
that, compared to their primary school counterparts, most pre-primary teachers have less working time 
on non-teaching tasks and on tasks not involving contact with children. The same study found only six 
countries reporting an equal time split between teaching and non-teaching tasks for both pre-primary 
teachers and primary teachers, although this configuration is projected to become increasingly prevalent 
across countries (OECD, 2017c[388]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
TALIS Starting Strong provides an opportunity to contribute to the currently limited evidence base 
regarding organisational climate and working conditions in ECEC settings. Furthermore, the data 
collected in the survey will allow for an examination of the relationship between different indicators of 
organisational climate and working conditions (e.g., working hours and stress, organisational climate, 
and staff engagement). 
Cross-national comparisons and analyses can highlight the policy environment in different countries and 
surface what is needed to help them improve working environments for their ECEC staff. Given the 
previously discussed association between organisational climate, working conditions, and ECEC setting 
quality, such efforts are also likely to contribute towards improving ECEC provision across countries. 
The indicators and dimensions concerned with ECEC setting climate can be summarised as follows. 
• 
Facilitation of and opportunities for continuous professional development and learning 
• 
Distributed leadership 

60    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
• 
Number of working hours 
• 
Contractual stability 
• 
Level of satisfaction with salary 
• 
Time spent on different tasks that are part of job duties 
• 
Sources of work stress linked to organisational processes and structures 
• 
Staff beliefs about priority areas for improving working conditions through targeted spending 
Cross-content area analyses 
Given evidence linking organisational climate, working conditions, and job satisfaction, TALIS Starting 
Strong can explore this association. Analyses can also examine the relationship of organisational 
climate and working conditions with various aspects of structural and process quality mentioned in this 
document. For instance, the extent to which organisational climate and working conditions relate to staff-
child interactions and the classroom/playroom climate would be of interest. 
Evidence has also described the important role that ECEC leaders play in shaping the organisational 
climate. TALIS Starting Strong may explore how various aspects of ECEC setting leadership relate to 
working conditions and other features of the organisational climate. Finally, examining these dimensions 
as part of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 provides continuity with the TALIS 2024 survey, thus making it 
possible to explore similarities and differences in organisational climate and working conditions across 
different levels of education. 
Stakeholder relations 
ECEC do not operate in isolation and often interact with stakeholders to enhance children’s 
development, well-being and learning both within and outside the ECEC setting. According to Hooge et 
al. (2012[389]), there are four groups of educational stakeholders. The primary stakeholders in education 
are children and families, followed by a second group formed by educators and other staff, which are 
considered as internal stakeholders. Institutions that allow the operation of the ECEC programmes such 
as municipalities, supervisors, educational ministry and local educational authorities are considered as 
vertical stakeholders, the third group. The fourth group of stakeholders refer to all other persons, groups 
and institutions that are interested in ECEC in one way or another, such as: community members and 
organizations, other educational providers (schools, universities, nurseries), social and health services 
and cultural organisations. This last group is considered to be horizontal stakeholders. This last group 
is considered to be horizontal stakeholders. 
Stakeholder relations and co-operation with ECEC services may facilitate children’s development, well-
being, and learning (Sim et al., 2019[22]), as children benefit from consistent communication between 
families and ECEC settings, comprehensive services and seamless transitions.. Good stakeholder 
relations can also help build competent systems that are able to adapt and respond to changing 
circumstances in the longer term (OECD, 2020a[390]; Pramling Samuelsson, Wagner and Eriksen 
Ødegaard, 2020[391]). 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Research shows that ECEC provision benefits from working with other services (such as family support, 
social services, and health services), engaging with parents and caregivers, and involving the entire 
range of ECEC stakeholders in a coordinated manner (Barnes et al., 2018[392]; Haslip and Gullo, 
2018[182]; Urban, Cardani and Romer, 2018[393]; OECD, 2019f[394]). However, in alignment with the goals 
of TALIS Starting Strong 2024, this sub-section focuses on the relationship of ECEC settings with two 

   61 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
of the four aforementioned stakeholder groups: families (group 1) and other institutions interested in 
ECEC, such as social and health services and other educational institutions (group 4). 
The first and most important stakeholders to connect with are families (stakeholder group 1). ECEC 
settings interact with families by promoting and facilitating parental involvement in their children’s 
learning and by engaging them in the setting’s life. The OECD (2012a[173]) reports that parental 
involvement in ECEC can indirectly influence child development, well-being, and learning; similar 
findings were reported by the Effective Provision of Pre-School Education [EPPE; Siraj-Blatchford et al. 
(2004[395])]. The relationship between parents and ECEC staff is of particular interest, given 
observational evidence of a correlation between quality of staff-parent interactions and children’s school 
attendance and socioemotional development; this correlation is even more pronounced in ethnic 
minority children (Zulauf‐McCurdy and Zinsser, 2021[396]; Zulauf-McCurdy and Loomis, 2023[397]). 
For instance, ECEC settings that have the same educational aims as parents, and that hold regular 
reporting and discussions with parents about their children’s progress, tend to see favourable socio-
cognitive outcomes for children (Epstein, 2001[398]; Siraj-Blatchford et al., 2004[395]). Research has also 
found that parental involvement in ECEC enhances parents’ understanding of appropriate educational 
practices and hence improves children’s literacy outcomes (Cooter et al., 1999[399]; Bryant, Peisner-
Feinberg and Miller-Johnson, 2000[400]). 
Combining formal and informal strategies to engage and communicate with parents may be beneficial, 
with informal communication potentially leading to greater parental satisfaction with children’s ECEC 
experiences (Gjerustad et al., 2020[280]). Given the increased diversity in the groups of children ECEC 
settings will need to adapt the ways they engage with parents. Examples of strategies to better engage 
parents with immigrant backgrounds include translating materials into different languages spoken by 
affiliated families, using translators for parent events and employing bilingual staff (Norheim and Moser, 
2020[401]). 
Digital technologies can also be used to strengthen the relationships between parents and ECEC staff 
(Lee, 2016[402]). Leaders can use digital technologies (e.g., text messages, social networks), or at least 
promote their use, to make communication with parents and guardians more efficient, and to ensure 
that information about their child is transmitted to both parents, in cases where the child’s parents may 
be divorced, for example (Schleicher, 2019[183]). 
Co-operation and communication with other educational institutions are important as well. For instance, 
children’s experiences while transitioning within ECEC and onto primary education can be made 
smoother by improving communication and co-ordination between practitioners across educational 
levels (Little, Cohen-Vogel and Curran, 2016[403]; Haciibrahimoglu and Kargin, 2017[404]). When ECEC 
settings and schools work together, they are better able to ensure consistency and continuity in 
children’s education, as well as design programmes that leverage their shared knowledge, skills, needs, 
capabilities, and experiences (Pianta and Kraft-Sayre, 2003[405]). 
However, co-operation between ECEC settings and primary schools remains a policy challenge for 
many countries. This is an especially important concern, since transitions between levels of education 
can be stressful for children or even impede their progress, if they are not handled with care (OECD, 
2006[268]). Furthermore, research shows that patterns of behaviour and achievement established during 
the transition period can influence children’s future academic and social trajectory (Dockett and Perry, 
2004[406]). 
ECEC and primary schools may implement different strategies to facilitate children’s transitions such 
as, including but not limited to: (a) staff and teachers exchanging information on children’s prior 
educational experience and development; (b) children and families visiting school facilities before 
enrolling in a primary school; (c) primary school teachers visiting children at ECEC settings; (d) sharing 

62    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
activities between ECEC settings and primary schools; and (e) adapting pedagogical practices (Shuey 
et al., 2019[407]). 
For instance, using flexible and adaptive approaches to pedagogy, monitoring, and assessment can 
help ECEC settings meet the learning needs of children in transition-year groups (OECD, 2019f[394]; 
Skolverket, 2019[408]). These approaches are also effective for smoothing transitions for children with 
special educational needs (Nuske et al., 2018[409]). In addition, establishing links between ECEC and 
school curricula can also help make transitions smoother (Shuey et al., 2019[407]). 
Beyond schools, there is a larger shift towards more integrated service delivery, driven by a growing 
awareness of the advantages of a more coordinated system of care and support for young children and 
their families (Barnes et al., 2018[392]). Integrating education, primary healthcare, nutrition, children’s 
rights, social cohesion, and equality into a coherent care system is thought to improve service delivery, 
resourcing, and professional development, which in turn improves services provided to children and 
their families (Urban, Cardani and Romer, 2018[393]). Indeed, it has been shown that effective 
engagement with stakeholders beyond education, such as social service and health service providers, 
are critical to ensuring children’s overall well-being (Barnes et al., 2018[392]). 
However, creating an integrated system can be difficult. Services relevant to children are spread across 
education, health, and social welfare systems, which are grounded in different disciplines and often work 
in silos (Barnes et al., 2018[392]; Urban, Cardani and Romer, 2018[393]). Nevertheless, many countries 
and states have made efforts to develop integrated early childhood policies, with the goal of offering 
broad-based, coordinated services for young children and families by way of inter-agency collaboration. 
In the US, there are at least 10 states working with the Build Initiative. The Build initiative partners with 
state authorities to promote a comprehensive early childhood system focused on early learning, family 
engagement and support, nutrition, health and mental health, and special needs (BUILD Initiative, 
n.d.[410]). In Latin America, Uruguay (Ministerio de Desarrollo Social, 2018[411]), Colombia (De Cero a 
Siempre, 2014[412]) and Chile (Saavedra, 2015[413]), serve as examples of strong integrated early 
childhood policies. 
The policies and initiatives described above require ECEC settings to work closely with health care 
providers and social services to ensure that children receive vaccinations, health check-ups, 
developmental screening and monitoring, early learning interventions, social subsidies, and services for 
special needs, among others. It is critical for involved agencies to coordinate and collaborate not only in 
delivering these services but also in monitoring children’s outcomes and trajectories. While beneficial 
for all children, inter-agency collaboration is especially critical for children who are at risk of being 
neglected or abused or who are receiving insufficient health care (Barnett and Masse, 2007[414]; Temple 
and Reynolds, 2007[415]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Within content area analyses 
Multiple examples of successful integration across systems have been documented in the literature, and 
that their success is at least partly contingent on country-specific contexts (Barnes et al., 2018[392]). 
Given this cross-country variability, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is well-placed to describe how ECEC 
settings in different countries work across agencies to support children, as well as explore how country-
specific contextual factors may influence these efforts. Furthermore, by obtaining setting-level 
information, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 provides insight into how ECEC setting leaders facilitate inter-
agency collaboration amidst varying policy contexts. 
The indicators and dimensions of stakeholder relations are listed below: 
• 
Relationships with parents or guardians 

   63 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
• 
Relationships with health and social services 
• 
Relationships with other education institutions (transition to other education levels or primary 
school). 
Cross-content area analyses 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 also has the potential to explore the association between stakeholder 
relations (e.g., parental engagement, outreach to other stakeholders – health and social services-, 
transition to primary schools) and group characteristics (e.g., socio-economic status or vulnerability- risk 
level, diversity), and ECEC staff and leader characteristics (e.g., experience, training, leadership). 
Assessing stakeholder relations provides the basis for understanding how the co-operation of ECEC 
settings with other stakeholders is linked to ECEC setting characteristics. 
Cross-cutting content areas 
Diversity, equity, and inclusion in ECEC settings 
Diversity, equity, and inclusion are terms often used interchangeably in the context of ECEC, but they 
represent distinct concepts. A number of recent studies have shed light on the distinctions between 
these closely related terms (Frost and Goldberg, 2019[416]; Shaeffer, 2019[417]; Liang and Cohrssen, 
2020[418]), which are the focus of this cross-cutting content area of TALIS Starting Strong 2024. 
Diversity relates to differences in the composition of children, families, and staff working within an ECEC 
setting. It covers a variety of biological, psychological, sociological, economic, geographical, and cultural 
characteristics of children and families. These characteristics include but are not limited to special 
educational needs, social class, neighbourhood, family income, ethnicity, migration status, cultural 
heritage, linguistic background, indigenous status, and family composition (one parent/two parent 
families; lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, queer, and intersex (LGBTQI) parents (Santon-Chapman, 
Denning and Roorbach Jamison, 2008[419]; Petriwskyj, 2010[420]). An equitable ECEC system provides 
equal access to opportunity for all children, regardless of their personal and familial characteristics. 
Equity in ECEC can be achieved through configurations at different levels of the system, from larger-
scale policy decisions made at the national level, to setting-level interactions between children, peers, 
and staff, as well as in engagements with the child’s immediate community outside ECEC settings 
(Unterhalter, 2009[421]; Ainscow, 2020[422]). 
As outlined in the TALIS 2024 conceptual framework, policy issues that contribute to equity in education 
include access to: (a) effective teachers; (b) high-quality learning opportunities; (c) adequate learning 
resources; and (d) diverse peer groups (van Langen, Bosker and Dekkers, 2006[423]; Dupriez and 
Dumay, 2006[424]; Montt, 2011[425]). The extent to which staff and leaders within educational settings 
reflects the diversity of the student population can also shape children’s development, well-being, and 
learning (Dekkers, Bosker and Driessen, 2000[426]; Van de Werfhorst and Mijs, 2010[427]), especially in 
ECEC (Zinsser et al., 2019[428]; Lastikka, 2019[429]; OECD, 2020f[430]). 
Inclusion is seen as a basic value and aim of ECEC, but beyond that, inclusion and inclusive systems 
are also a means to meet the needs of increasingly diverse populations of children and achieve equity 
(Frost and Goldberg, 2019[416]; Shaeffer, 2019[417]). An inclusive ECEC system caters to the needs of all 
children, including but not limited to children from ethnolinguistic minority groups, children from non-
heteronormative family structures, and children with special learning or healthcare needs. For children 
with special learning or healthcare needs, inclusion is concerned with the extent to which they can 
participate in ECEC settings with their typically developing peers (National Academies of Sciences, 
2018[431]). For children from ethnic minority groups (e.g. children with an indigenous background), 

64    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
inclusion involves expanding the extent to which children’s different cultures are represented, as well as 
adapting care, teaching, and leadership practices to empower children from diverse cultures. These 
inclusive policies and practices can be put in place through concerted action across ECEC staff, settings, 
and systems. 
Indeed, ECEC has the potential to mitigate inequalities of opportunities associated with children’s 
ethnicity, migrant or refugee status, educational needs, location, or family composition (Broekhuizen 
et al., 2019[432]; OECD, 2019c[177]). There is evidence from both randomised controlled trials and 
observational studies that ECEC has the potential to improve the life chances of children from 
disadvantaged families [e.g., Schweinhart & Weikart (1997[433]); Melhuish et al. (2008[434]); Dearing et al. 
(2009[435]); Camilli et al. (2010[436]); Barnett (2011[437]); Zachrisson & Dearing (2015[438])]. Similar results 
have been reported by studies regarding the impact of ECEC on indigenous groups: preschool 
education has a number of long-term benefits for indigenous children, especially when it comes to 
learning and development (Elek et al., 2020[439]). 
However, there are challenges associated with providing equitable access to quality ECEC. Changes in 
governance structures – such as increased privatisation of ECEC in some countries – can bring 
challenges in ensuring equitable access and quality (Wright, 2011[440]). There are also notable 
differences in access to ECEC between urban and rural locations, highlighting geographical inequalities 
(Temple, 2009[441]; UNICEF, 2020[442]). Moreover, there is a growing body of literature that underscores 
the need for ECEC staff to have a range of linguistic and cultural competencies to meet the needs of an 
increasingly diverse population of children (Zinsser et al., 2019[428]). 
Despite these challenges, it is acknowledged that all children should be given equitable access to 
opportunities to promote their development, well-being, and learning (Nxumalo and Adair, 2019[443]). 
Therefore, it is necessary to take an equity perspective to inform research on factors that affect children’s 
development, well-being, and learning (Iruka, 2020[444]). Several factors have been noted as being 
critical to achieving equity and promoting diversity in ECEC settings, including but not limited to ECEC 
staff (Rosenberg, 2020[445]), and national curricula (Melvin, Landsberg and Kagan, 2020[446]). 
Theoretical and empirical background 
The expansion of ECEC in many countries has led to a rapid increase in the number of children receiving 
early education. This expansion – alongside changes in the composition of populations due to factors 
like increased migration – has resulted in ECEC settings serving more culturally, linguistically, and 
socioeconomically diverse groups of children (Bader et al., 2019[365]; Schleicher, 2019[183]; González-
Sancho et al., 2023[447]; Norheim and Moser, 2020[401]). Furthermore, non-heteronormative families 
(such as LGBTQI families), families built through adoption, and multicultural families have increased in 
prevalence, with many families characterised by intersectional identities (Frost and Goldberg, 2019[416]). 
However, unequal access to quality ECEC remains a pervasive issue. While some countries have 
implemented targeted policies to boost attendance of children from disadvantaged and minority families 
in higher-quality ECEC settings (Leseman et al., 2017[448]), socioeconomically disadvantaged children
