remain disproportionately underrepresented in high-quality settings (Petitclerc et al., 2017[449]; Aguiar 
and Aguiar, 2020[450]). 
This is true regardless of whether countries have market-based and targeted programmes [e.g., in the 
US: see Fuller et al. (1996[451])] or universal, subsidised access to ECEC (Sibley et al., 2015[452]). 
Furthermore, evidence from the US suggests, that in some contexts, children from socioeconomically 
disadvantaged families tend to enrol in settings of lower quality, compared to their more affluent peers 
(Dowsett, Huston and Imes, 2008[453]; González-Sancho et al., 2023[447]). Research also indicates that 
ECEC settings serving high-SES communities, and communities with a low proportion of families from 
minority backgrounds, may be more attractive to highly qualified and effective teachers; this, in turn, 

   65 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
perpetuates the link between socio-economic disadvantage and a lower-quality ECEC provision (Reid 
et al., 2015[454]). 
Studies also suggest that peer group composition influences children’s development (Justice et al., 
2011[455]; Ribeiro, Zachrisson and Dearing, 2017[456]; Ribeiro and Zachrisson, 2017[457]). The extent to 
which children in ECEC settings are segregrated by key characteristics such as race, ethnicity, or 
economic status is another important consideration. For example, research in the US indicates that 
ECEC programs are more segregated than early primary classrooms in terms of the race composition 
of the enrolled children (Greenberg and Monarrez, 2019[458]). 
Children in such segregated ECEC settings do not experience the potential benefits of more integrated 
early childhood environments (Reid et al., 2015[454]). For instance, evidence from the Netherlands 
indicates that socioeconomically disadvantaged children who participated in mixed-background child 
groups gained more in literacy and reading than those in socioeconomically homogenous, targeted child 
groups (De Haan et al., 2013[227]). Consequently, children from socio-economically disadvantaged 
families, and from ethnically diverse families, are at an even greater disadvantage as they often attend 
settings alongside children from similar backgrounds (Becker and Schober, 2017[459]). 
Socio-economic factors also appear to interact with other demographic characteristics. For instance, a 
US study showed that more affluent schools tended to be more inclusive towards non-heteronormative 
families (Bishop and Atlas, 2013[460]). Furthermore, the degree to which the ECEC setting climate is 
accepting of non-heteronormative family structures has been shown by a number of studies to have an 
impact on children’s educational outcomes [e.g., Peter et al. (2016[461]); Liang & Cohrssen (2020[418])]. 
These findings further highlight the need to design ECEC development, well-being, and learning 
environments that address the needs of children from a wide range of backgrounds. 
It is also important to consider whether the composition of ECEC staff within a setting – especially staff 
who work directly with children – with respect to the demographic composition of the child group is 
associated with differences in children’s development, well-being, and learning outcomes (Bernhard 
et al., 1996[462]; Zinsser et al., 2019[428]). For instance, children’s families may find it easier to 
communicate with ECEC staff from similar racial, ethnic, cultural, linguistic, or migrant backgrounds 
(Archambault, Côté and Raynault, 2019[463]; Lastikka, 2019[429]; Bergroth and Hansell, 2020[464]). 
Curricular content and teacher preparation have also been identified as contributing to inclusive ECEC. 
For instance, using reading materials that depict non-heteronormative characters help develop very 
young children’s gender literacy (Averett, Hegde and Smith, 2017[465]). However, introducing more 
gender-inclusive instructional practices is not enough; this should be accompanied by corresponding 
teacher preparation since this would, to an extent, determine whether such changes will succeed. 
Unfortunately, this is not always the case. For instance, a US study found that while pre-service ECEC 
teachers held positive attitudes towards children from non-heteronormative family structures, they felt 
inadequately prepared to cater to those children’s needs (Averett and Hegde, 2012[466]). Furthermore, 
research on the effectiveness of gender-inclusive pre-service training interventions remains limited. 
Nonetheless, there is some evidence that such interventions can influence early-career teachers’ 
attitudes towards non-heteronormative children and families, as well as the extent to which they feel 
prepared to address the said population’s needs (Kintner-Duffy et al., 2012[467]). 
Policies regarding the inclusion of children with special education needs have also been shifting. The 
last few decades have seen policies move towards serving typically developing children and children 
with special educational needs in the same educational settings (National Academies of Sciences, 
2018[431]). This shift has also been seen in ECEC settings, with the expectation that staff and leaders 
will have the knowledge and skills needed to work with children with a wide range of development, well-
being, and learning needs. However, while ECEC settings are becoming increasingly diverse, the 

66    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
availability of relevant support for children, staff, and leaders in ECEC settings is likely to vary across 
countries and jurisdictions, owing to differences in policy and resourcing. 
Given the increasing diversity in ECEC settings, identifying ‘diversity gaps’ in ECEC provision and 
exploring how ECEC can promote equity have become pressing research areas (Duncan and 
Magnuson, 2013[468]; Sim et al., 2019[22]). It is also of high policy relevance to identify across countries 
and jurisdictions the extent to which children from different backgrounds are clustered in ECEC, and 
whether and where settings with substantial numbers of minority group children have lower quality than 
their counterparts. TALIS Starting Strong can contribute further evidence in this area. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
Diversity and equity was a cross-cutting content area in TALIS Starting Strong 2018 and remains of high 
relevance for the second cycle. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 retains this focus while also attending to 
issues of inclusion. 
TALIS Starting Strong can examine how pedagogical practices might vary depending on 
sociodemographic characteristics at the level of children, families, or ECEC settings – hence revealing 
any disparities. Information regarding the sociodemographic diversity of ECEC staff can also be 
captured. TALIS Starting Strong can also explore the extent to which pedagogical practices are adapted 
to increasingly diverse groups of children, and whether ECEC staff needs for professional development 
and perceived readiness to respond to this diversity and to support individual children are related to the 
contexts in which they work. 
 As a cross-country study, TALIS Starting Strong can reveal within- and between-country differences in 
pedagogical practices, staff attitudes, and professional development related to diversity, equity, and 
inclusion. 
Like TALIS 2018, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 places dual emphasis on socioeconomic equity and 
multiculturalism. Relevant indicators include: 
• 
Composition of children in the ECEC setting 
• 
Composition of children in a target group 
• 
Staff linguistic background 
• 
Approaches to diversity and inclusion 
• 
Content of professional development and need for further development regarding diversity, 
equity and inclusion 
• 
Beliefs and attitudes towards diversity, equity and inclusion 
• 
Self-efficacy relating to diversity, equity and inclusion practices. 
 
Approaches to comprehensive and continuous child supports 
This content area covers two distinct but interrelated elements. Comprehensive and continuous 
approaches to education refer to: 
i. 
the support children receive to facilitate their development and learning across a range of 
areas, including but not limited to creative thinking, socio-emotional skills, cognitive skills, 
environmental awareness, and physical health; and 
ii. 
seamless transitions. 

   67 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
ECEC serves as children’s first formal opportunity to develop critical skills that can support not only their 
success and engagement in later educational stages but also their feelings of belonging in society. In 
other words, ECEC is an important early determinant of children’s developmental trajectory. 
ECEC supports development of transversal skills (e.g., creative thinking, socio-emotional competence). 
Fostering pro-social attitudes and behaviours also form part of comprehensive ECEC. Hence, issues of 
social justice – such as climate, health, equality, and diversity – can also figure into the areas of 
development, well-being, and learning supported by ECEC provision (Wray‐Lake and Syvertsen, 
2011[469]; Black et al., 2017[470]). Participating in social justice-oriented activities can support children’s 
feelings of connection to their communities and the environment, while also facilitating their transitions 
through different stages of learning. 
Seamless transitions refer to the way in which ECEC is seen as part of an integrated education and 
care system, with ECEC settings acting in partnership across ECEC stages and onwards to subsequent 
levels of education (Ucus, Acar and Raikes, 2017[471]). These partnerships start with children’s transition 
from the home environment to ECEC as they first enter a non-parental care environment. 
Integrated approaches that aim to leverage expertise and knowledge to promote better outcomes for 
children are gaining policy traction (Rentzou and Daglas, 2018[472]). This is supported by a wider body 
of literature that sees ECEC as being more than a means to prepare children for school, instead 
positioning it as a fundamental mechanism that supports children’s development, well-being, and 
learning and allows them to take full advantage of their potential for the future (Hayes and Filipović, 
2018[473]). 
Theoretical and empirical background 
There is growing consensus regarding the need to view ECEC as part of a holistic system that also 
encompasses areas traditionally associated with the domains of health and social care, as well as other 
stages of education (Goren Niron, 2013[474]; Vaknin, 2020[475]). This holistic view contrasts with 
approaches that see ECEC as operating in isolation from other services and delivered entirely 
separately from subsequent levels of education [that is, as opposed to being the first stage of a 
coordinated education system; Urban et al. (2018[393]); Van Laere & Vandenbroeck (2018[476]); OECD 
(2019f[394])]. Supporters of this more holistic view highlight ECEC’s potential to support children’s agency 
in broader societal issues, such as climate and the environment, nutrition, and digital technologies 
(OECD, 2019c[177]). This can be seen in ECEC curricula in different countries, which now incorporate 
elements of environmental education and education for sustainable development. 
For instance, in Finland, practical guidance on how to create sustainable development programmes at 
kindergarten level has been made available (Siraj-Blatchford and Pramling-Samuelsson, 2016[477]). In 
Korea, the national curriculum for three to five-year-olds has been revised to incorporate principles of 
green education, aiming to promote transformative and systemic thinking in children to create 
awareness of sustainable development issues (Park et al., 2016). Indeed, the integration of sustainable 
development with education is gaining prominence in policy, as evidenced by its incorporation in the 
United Nations Education Sustainable Goals (SDG 4) (UNESCO, 2012[478]; Ødegaard, 2021[479]). 
ECEC settings can also teach children about the importance of good nutrition and provide access to, as 
well opportunities for modelling healthy eating habits, which can prevent later onset of nutrition-related 
issues like overweight and obesity (OECD, 2017b[480]). This is a pressing issue, given the rising 
incidence of childhood obesity in some OECD member states (Martin and Karoly, 2016[481]; OECD, 
2019a[189]). 
Facilitating transitions between ECEC settings and onwards to subsequent levels of education is another 
vital function of ECEC provision (Schleicher, 2019[183]; Shuey et al., 2019[407]). Successful transitions 
can support children’s development, well-being, and learning over the long term (Schleicher, 2019[183]). 

68    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
For example, leading up to transition periods (e.g., between ECEC settings, or from ECEC settings to 
school), staff across settings (or schools) can share knowledge and experiences regarding children and 
families, as well as provide children and families with more information on the new setting or school. 
This kind of information-sharing promotes continuity and progression in children’s learning and 
development trajectories (Schleicher, 2019[183]). 
Taking a comprehensive approach can help smooth transitions across stages of ECEC (i.e. from ISCED 
01 to ISCED 02) and from ECEC to primary education (Jay and Knaus, 2018[482]; OECD, 2019d[483]; 
Schleicher, 2019[183]; Shuey et al., 2019[407]). For instance, education systems may establish links 
between the ECEC curriculum and that of subsequent education levels (Schleicher, 2019[183]; Shuey 
et al., 2019[407]). Using flexible and adaptive monitoring and assessment methods that make it easy for 
ECEC staff adjust their pedagogy according to children’s individual needs can also support children in 
transition year groups (OECD, 2019f[394]; Skolverket, 2019[408]). However, research suggests that 
relevant professional development opportunities, which could help support this kind of continuity, 
communication and monitoring, are more developed for kindergarten/preschool staff than for staff 
working with children under the age of three, and limited for home-based ECEC staff (Schleicher, 
2019[183]). This situation limits the ability of some of these ECEC settings to develop best practice and 
can impede efforts for different parts of the ECEC system to work together effectively. 
In sum, high-quality ECEC requires coordinated efforts both within and across sectors (Rebello Britto, 
Engle and Super, 2013[484]). To make such coordinated efforts possible, developing a shared, 
intersectoral understanding and vision of holistic child development is imperative (Black et al., 2017[470]). 
However, the degree to which different countries and jurisdictions take a comprehensive approach to 
ECEC varies. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 can help build a picture of current practice within and across 
countries, allowing for a stronger understanding of how ECEC settings see themselves with respect to 
the larger child-care system. 
Analytical potential and indicators 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 captures aspects of comprehensive and continuous approaches to child 
supports by exploring staff professional beliefs about children’s development, well-being, and learning. 
For example, the survey asks ECEC staff about their beliefs regarding the importance of developing 
different competence areas (e.g., self-care skills, motor skills, socioemotional skills) and their use of 
related practices to help develop those competences. 
ECEC staff are also asked about how they assess and monitor children, how they adapt their teaching 
to children needs, and how this informs transitions of children onto subsequent education levels. Staff 
and leader relationships with stakeholders outside the ECEC setting (e.g., other education levels or 
health and social protection services), and information on ECEC setting characteristics, such as setting 
location, (e.g., co-location with primary schools, or proximity to other services) are also relevant to 
ensuring seamless transitions for children. 
The comprehensive and continuous approaches to child supports and transitions in the survey include: 
• 
Beliefs about promoting holistic development in children (e.g., facilitating pro-social 
behaviour and self-regulation alongside skills such as literacy and numeracy) 
• 
Beliefs and practices pertaining to the role of ECEC in developing early environmental 
awareness and responsibility in young children 
• 
Relationships with other stakeholders (e.g., health, social protection and other child-care 
services) 
• 
Staff use of monitoring and assessment of children to facilitate transitions 

   69 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Fostering competent ECEC settings 
Fostering competent ECEC settings refers to ECEC settings’ ability to respond to change in an efficient 
and effective manner. This means ensuring that settings, leaders, staff, and families can cope with 
disruptions caused by events like economic and resource instability, health emergencies, and natural 
disasters. The COVID-19 pandemic may have brought this issue to the fore (Pramling Samuelsson, 
Wagner and Eriksen Ødegaard, 2020[391]; Mantovani et al., 2021[485]), but disruptions caused by natural 
disasters have also been found to be detrimental to children’s learning (Mutch, 2015[486]). To cope with 
such disruptions, ECEC staff and leaders should develop relevant competencies through their pre-
service training and continuing professional development. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
Competent ECEC settings are critical for ensuring continuity and quality of provision, particularly in 
situations that bring about significant upheaval, change, or disruption. This has become acutely relevant, 
following the COVID-19 pandemic, which prompted the unprecedented, mass closure of private and 
public educational institutions. Several studies have highlighted the pandemic’s adverse impact on 
children, with those living in poverty being disproportionately affected; this has aggravated inequalities 
(United Nations, 2020[487]; Pascal et al., 2020[488]). 
During the pandemic, ECEC settings had to quickly adapt and respond to new and frequently changing 
demands related to hygiene, social distancing, and test-and-trace protocols (Atiles et al., 2021[489]). In 
addition, ECEC practitioners had to adopt new ways of liaising with parents and supporting children, 
including moving to online education (Pavlenko and Pavlenko, 2020[490]; Atiles et al., 2021[489]). 
For instance, educational settings can act as centres for disaster response and recovery and for 
supporting emotional, post-traumatic recovery (Mutch, 2015[486]; Le Brocque et al., 2017[491]). These 
kinds of events can happen to countries at all levels of economic development, but their negative impact 
may be more salient in lower-income countries (Luby and Arthur, 2019[492]). Given the role of ECEC 
settings in disaster response and recovery, ECEC staff and leaders should receive adequate preparation 
to fulfil the accompanying responsibilities. 
System-wide disruptions can also adversely affect the financial resources and organisational resilience 
of ECEC settings. For instance, COVID-19 drove many childcare providers out of business due to 
mandated closures and/or resulting bottlenecks in their funding streams (Vesoulis, 2020[493]; Viner et al., 
2020[494]). Similarly, during the Great Recession, national per-pupil spending in US public schools fell by 
roughly 7 percent, leading to larger income- and race-related achievement gaps (Jackson et al., 2021). 
As such, the levels of state support that educational settings receive in times of crisis or severe disruption 
is an important consideration when examining the competence of ECEC settings. 
Several factors have been identified as playing a part in the development of competent and resilient 
ECEC settings. Good stakeholder relations can help build competent settings that are able to adapt and 
respond to changing circumstances over the long term (OECD, 2020c[495]; Pramling Samuelsson, 
Wagner and Eriksen Ødegaard, 2020[391]). This includes relationships with parents, caregivers, and 
practitioners in the wider community (such as health visitors and social workers). 
Practitioner training and development to support distance teaching and learning has also been noted as 
a necessary factor (Atiles et al., 2021[489]). Leaders in ECEC settings could also benefit from developing 
skills and allotting time towards building financial management strategies that would making their ECEC 
setting more resilient in the face of disruption (Muijs et al., 2004[337]; Hujala and Eskelinen, 2013[496]; 
Hujala et al., 2016[497]). 
Attracting and retaining qualified staff is also critical to an ECEC setting’s ability to cope with adverse 
circumstances (Logan, Cumming and Wong, 2020[373]; Fenech et al., 2021[498]). Therefore, better 

70    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
understanding how and the extent to which ECEC leaders work towards ensuring good working 
conditions for ECEC staff, such as a positive organisational climate and support structures to help staff 
cope with work-related stress, is a prime concern (OECD, 2020d[499]; OECD, 2020e[500]; Pramling 
Samuelsson, Wagner and Eriksen Ødegaard, 2020[391]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
The evidence presented above suggests that strong pedagogical and administrative leadership is crucial 
for fostering resilient ECEC systems. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 can shed light on practices that can 
help build ECEC staff and leaders’ capacity to respond to different types of crises. For example, the 
survey explores how leaders manage setting resources, as well as the types of training received (or 
needed) by leaders to enable them to effectively manage their settings’ resources. 
Given the importance of organisational climate and working conditions to ECEC staff’s fulfilment of their 
responsibilities, the survey also asks ECEC staff about their beliefs regarding their setting leadership 
(including the extent to which they feel supported by their leader), as well as their job satisfaction and 
sources of work stress. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 also directly asks respondents about their 
perception of their own ability to react to system disruptions. 
The following indicators and dimensions are associated with fostering competent ECEC settings: 
• 
Type and content of professional development 
• 
Staff needs for further professional development 
• 
Organisational climate for staff learning 
• 
Distributed leadership 
• 
Beliefs about leader and pedagogical leadership 
• 
Satisfaction with autonomy, ECEC setting, work environment and working conditions 
• 
Sources of work stress 
• 
Parent or guardian engagement 
• 
Relationships with other stakeholders (e.g., parents or guardians, social services, schools, 
community settings) 
• 
Opportunities to develop collective competence (in-service) 
• 
Setting resources 
• 
Resources for professional development of ECEC staff 
• 
Autonomy and capacity to respond to changing regulations 
Digital technologies to support staff and leaders 
The digital revolution has influenced all levels of educational provision, including ECEC. However, while 
its use in education was more readily accepted in the context of older learners, its implementation in 
ECEC has been met with more scrutiny and scepticism (Burns and Gottschalk, 2020[294]). Digital 
technologies are becoming increasingly present, and there is a need to improve understanding of how 
they can be used productively in the ECEC context (Marklund, 2019[171]; OECD, 2019b[501]; Schleicher, 
2019[183]; Burns and Gottschalk, 2020[294]; OECD, 2023[244]). 
TALIS Starting Strong recognises that using digital technology in ECEC settings can benefit children. It 
could increase children’s engagement and prepare them for the realities of a digital society (Zabatiero 
et al., 2018[295]; Schleicher, 2019[183]; OECD, 2019c[177]; Mertala, 2019[502]; Burns and Gottschalk, 
2020[294]). However, despite potential benefits, there are risks and concerns. Among these are the digital 
competencies of ECEC staff and questions regarding the privacy of children, families, and ECEC staff 

   71 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
(Zabatiero et al., 2018[295]; Schleicher, 2019[183]; Burns and Gottschalk, 2020[294]). The COVID-19 
pandemic brought on pressure to use digital technologies with younger children in support of distance 
learning, despite limited knowledge on how to use digital tools for ECEC in a developmentally 
appropriate manner and the consequences for staff practices and children’s learning (Fleer, 2020[503]; 
Atiles et al., 2021[489]). 
While all these elements are relevant to ECEC, the focus of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is on digital 
technologies as a resource for ECEC staff and leaders. This includes how technology can facilitate staff 
and ECEC leaders’ initial training and professional development, and its uses for professional practices, 
such as collaboration (Murcia, Campbell and Aranda, 2018[172]; Zabatiero et al., 2018[295]; Burns and 
Gottschalk, 2020[294]). The appropriateness of digital technologies for use in delivering ECEC to young 
children is not covered. 
Theoretical and empirical background 
The content of professional development and training can shape practitioners’ opinions on the use of 
digital technologies in ECEC settings, which can in turn influence their decision to incorporate (or not 
incorporate) digital tools in their pedagogical practice (Kerckaert, Vanderlinde and van Braak, 2015[504]). 
After all, ECEC staff who are more confident about using digital technologies are more likely to use it in 
their practice (Murcia, Campbell and Aranda, 2018[172]). One Australian study found that although 
teachers could be hesitant to incorporate digital tools in their pedagogical practices, some did use them 
for lesson planning, finding teaching resources or observation/reporting purposes (Dwyer, Jones and 
Rosas, 2019[505]). It is also worth noting that while ECEC practitioners readily use digital devices in their 
personal life, they are hesitant to use them for purposes of play-based learning (Palaiologou, 2016[506]; 
Aldhafeeri, Palaiologo and Folorunsho, 2016[507]). These findings highlight the importance of exploring 
professional beliefs and self-efficacy as they relate to digital technologies (Zabatiero et al., 2018[295]). 
A recent study examining how ECEC staff in the US and several Latin American countries coped with 
disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic identified a lack of preparation for distance teaching as 
a key challenge (Atiles et al., 2021[489]). Similarly, TALIS Starting Strong 2018 found that ECEC staff 
were less confident about using digital technologies to support children’s learning, compared to other 
areas of their work (OECD, 2020b[99]). Indeed, despite the benefits of using digital technologies, a lot of 
ECEC staff feel they lack knowledge on how to use digital technologies to support children’s 
development, well-being, and learning (Murcia, Campbell and Aranda, 2018[172]; Marklund, 2019[171]; 
OECD, 2020b[99]). Based on these findings, the OECD recommended that training on the effective use 
of digital technologies in ECEC delivery be included in continuing professional development 
programmes (OECD, 2020b[99]). Technology-mediated pre-service education and continuing 
professional development should not only focus on technical aspects, but also on pedagogical elements 
that can support meaningful staff learning, teaching and quality monitoring of their practice (Fernández-
Batanero et al., 2020[508]). 
Digital technologies can also facilitate the delivery of professional development. Since digital 
technologies can facilitate peer-to-peer communication, they can help convene ECEC staff – prompting 
reflection, collaboration, and knowledge-sharing (Cherrington and Thornton, 2013[509]; Marklund, 
2019[171]; Campbell-Barr et al., 2020[510]; OECD, 2023[244]). ECEC staff can also use online self-
assessment tools to monitor their own learning needs and progress (Murcia, Campbell and Aranda, 
2018[172]). Moreover, digital technologies can facilitate feedback. For example, MyTeachingPartner is a 
video-based professional development coaching programme; ECEC staff receive individual feedback 
on video-recordings of their practice that they create and upload on their own time (Gregory et al., 
2017[511]; Ansari and Pianta, 2018[512]). 
Digital technologies can also support ECEC staff collaboration. For instance, online platforms can 
enable ECEC professionals to connect with others within and across ECEC settings to establish 

72    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
communities of practice (Mertala, 2019[502]; Marklund, 2019[171]; Dwyer, Jones and Rosas, 2019[505]; 
OECD, 2023[244]). Furthermore, digital technology can facilitate communication between staff and 
children’s parents or guardians (Lee, 2016[402]). Leaders can use or promote the use of technology, such 
as text messages or social networks, to make sure that parents and guardians can efficiently access 
information and news about the ECEC setting and their child, and to ensure that information is 
transmitted to both parents, for example, in cases of divorce (Schleicher, 2019[183]; Oke, Butler and 
O’Neill, 2021[513]; OECD, 2023[244]). 
Analytical potential and indicators 
As discussed earlier, the content of initial training and continuing professional development can 
influence ECEC staff and leaders’ confidence in using digital technology and, subsequently, their 
decision to use (or not use) technology in their practice. Hence, TALIS Starting Strong 2024 asks ECEC 
staff and leaders about: (a) whether they had received training content around the use of digital 
technologies to facilitate their work with children; (b) how confident they feel about using digital 
technologies; (c) barriers to professional development related to digital technologies. The survey can 
also capture information on whether online digital tools are used to engage with parents or guardians. 
The following is a summary of the indicators and dimensions concerned with use of technology in ECEC 
that are explored in the survey: 
• 
Initial education received regarding the use of digital technologies 
• 
Continuing professional development received regarding the use of digital technologies 
• 
Self-efficacy in using digital technologies to support work with children 
• 
Barriers to participating in professional development related to digital technologies 
• 
Use of digital technologies for outreach to parents or guardians 
• 
Need for investment in digital technology infrastructure.

TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025

   109 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
The populations covered by TALIS Starting Strong 2024 encompass centre-based ISCED level 02 staff 
and all staff working with children under the age of 3 and are as comparable as possible across 
participating countries and jurisdictions. All early childhood educators, pre-primary teachers, kindergarten 
teachers, preschool teachers, and auxiliary staff taking part in pedagogical work within registered early 
childhood education and care settings, and their leaders, are included. 
Target populations and sampling 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 investigates two target populations: 
• 
Staff and leaders working in pre-primary ECEC centres (ISCED level 02). 
• 
Staff and leaders working in settings providing ECEC services for children under the age of 3. 
Although the core focus of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 is ISCED level 02, participating countries and 
jurisdictions can choose to include the population of registered provision for children under the age of 
3 years. TALIS Starting 2024 uses the same version of the leader questionnaire, staff questionnaire, and 
combined questionnaire for both target populations. A series of consultations with ECEC experts were 
conducted to ensure the applicability of all questionnaires to both ISCED level 02 ECEC centres and ECEC 
settings serving children under the age of three, as well as ECEC settings serving both target populations. 
Nevertheless, to acknowledge contextual differences across ECEC centres and settings, the combined 
questionnaire, which consists of questions taken from the staff questionnaire and the leader questionnaire, 
has two versions. One version was administered to small centres (with only one staff person) or home-
based settings while the other was used for leaders who also perform staff duties. 
Defining ECEC settings in TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
ECEC settings are defined as officially registered settings that provide ECEC programmes (i.e. formal 
education and care) for young children from birth up to entry into primary education – also defined as 
ISCED level 0. To be classified as an ‘ECEC setting’, a setting must provide educational activities for at 
least 2 hours per day and 100 days per year. 
ISCED level 02 settings provide education and care designed to support early development in preparation 
for participation in school and society, and usually accommodate children from age 3 to the start of primary 
education. ISCED level 02 is also often referred to as ‘pre-primary education’. 
The other target population is ECEC settings that: 1) accommodate children younger than 3 years of age; 
and 2) implement early childhood programmes that contribute to the well-being, development and learning 
of children in this age group. Those can be based in a traditional centre (e.g., a kindergarten or a crèche) 
5 Design of TALIS Starting Strong 
2024 

110    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
or in someone's home. Countries need not formally apply the ISCED level 01 definition to settings to 
participate in this module. 
Home-based settings were considered in scope when sampling services for children under the age of 3, 
as long as they are officially registered within the country’s early childhood education and care system and 
under the country’s or relevant jurisdiction’s regulations. In contrast, all home-based settings were 
considered out of scope when sampling ISCED level 02 ECEC centres. 
ECEC settings entirely devoted to children with special education needs are considered out of scope for 
the survey. However, staff members working with children with special education needs in regular ECEC 
settings are in scope. 
ECEC settings that accommodate both children in ISCED level 02 and children under the age of 3 (i.e. 
mixed age or integrated settings) belong to both target populations. 
Defining ECEC staff and leaders in TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
The target population of ECEC staff comprises all persons working regularly in a pedagogical way with 
children within registered ECEC settings. ECEC staff members are defined as persons who, as part of 
their regular duties in the target setting, provide learning opportunities or care. 
ECEC setting leaders are defined as the person with the most responsibility for the administrative, 
managerial, and/or pedagogical leadership in their ECEC setting. In some ECEC settings, the leader might 
also spend part of their time working with children. 
Staff members are in scope regardless of the number of hours they work with children from the respective 
target populations. Therefore, staff members working with children of both age groups belong de facto to 
both target populations. 
Leaders who also fulfil the definition of a staff member are included in both the staff and leader populations. 
Substitute and other emergency staff, as well as staff on long-term leave, are not part of the target 
populations. 
Overview of the sample design 
The objective of the survey is to obtain unbiased estimates of the parameters to be calculated during the 
analysis process for each of the two target populations. A suitable sampling strategy has been chosen to 
reflect this objective (see Figure 5.1 below). The samples must yield sufficient data and suitable indicators 
to allow for meaningful interpretations of the study results. The samples should also be sufficiently broad 
so that labour market and system-wide indicators could be used to draw valid inferences for policy analysis. 
The resulting data should contain sufficient detail so that ECEC setting-level data and indicators can policy 
discussion. This is required for both the staff and leader questionnaires, and for each target population. 
The samples for the main survey consist of a minimum of 180 ECEC settings per participating 
country/economy and target population. In ECEC settings with eight or more eligible staff members, at 
least eight staff members are selected; in settings with fewer eligible staff members, all of them are included 
in the sample. The leader is selected in each setting, in addition to these staff members. 

   111 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Figure 5.1. Overview of sampling design 
 
If countries and jurisdictions, for various reasons, are prevented from surveying all ECEC staff, they are 
allowed to exclude ECEC settings. However, a 5% threshold is adopted as an upper limit for exclusions 
within each target population. 
The sampling plan is a two-stage design, with ECEC settings as primary sampling units and staff as 
secondary sampling units. Depending on each country’s unique situation, ECEC settings are selected with 
a systematic random sampling approach, with probability proportional to size if an appropriate measure of 
size exists. 
The expected minimum participation rates are set at 75% of ECEC settings and 75% of staff from 
participating ECEC settings. An ECEC setting will be deemed to have participated if at least half of its 
sampled staff members complete the staff questionnaire. These requirements are the same as in the TALIS 
Starting Strong 2018 cycle. 
Overview of survey instruments and their development 
The TALIS Starting Strong instruments cover the themes presented in Figure 1 and discussed in detail in 
Section III. The TALIS Starting Strong 2024 questionnaires are based on: 
• 
A review of the proposed content areas and indicators for the survey to ensure that the 
variables, indicators, and content areas provide a logical basis for instrument development, 
giving consideration to completeness and coherence. 
• 
A review of the catalogue of existing questions compiled from the TALIS Starting Strong 2018 
and the TALIS 2013 and TALIS 2018 surveys, as well as other national and international 
studies, in order to assess their suitability for measuring constructs within the TALIS Starting 
Strong 2024 conceptual framework and to identify other possible sources of exemplary 
questions. 
• 
Newly developed questions for the development of the identified indicators and research 
questions. 
• 
Stakeholder feedback from the Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong 2024 and the ECEC 
Network. 
• 
A thorough review and revision of the questionnaires based on the pilot and field trial results. 

112    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Overview of survey operations 
As with many other large-scale international comparative surveys (e.g., TALIS), TALIS Starting Strong 
2024 includes three consecutive study phases: a pilot study, a field trial and the main survey (see 
Figure 5.2 below). To validate the quality and content of the survey instruments for the ECEC context, 
especially for newly developed and modified items, a pilot study was conducted across many participating 
countries and jurisdictions. 
Based on the positive experiences and results from TALIS 2018 and TALIS Starting Strong 2018, a 
qualitative approach was also selected for the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 pilot. Following this approach, 
feedback and comments from ECEC staff members and leaders of both TALIS Starting Strong 2024 target 
populations (ISCED Level 02 and settings for children under the age of 3) were captured through guided 
focus group discussions carried out in participating countries and jurisdictions. Field trial instruments were 
then prepared based on the results and feedback collected in the pilot study. 
Figure 5.2. General timeline of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
 
 
The objective of the field trial was to test the survey instruments and operational procedures in all 
participating countries and jurisdictions in preparation for the main survey. For the field trial, the minimum 
sample size per country or jurisdiction was set to 30 sampled ECEC settings per country, ideally providing 
data for 30 leaders and 240 ECEC staff members per country and target population (if all respondents 
completed their questionnaires). Each participating country or jurisdiction was required to implement the 
field trial according to standardised procedures before the main survey. Technical standards and 
corresponding quality control measures were developed for the TALIS 2024 programme, to ensure that 
TALIS Starting Strong is implemented in ways that will yield comparable data across participating countries 
and jurisdictions and across levels of education. 
The main survey data collection was conducted in two waves. A minimum sample of 1,200 ECEC staff 
members and their leaders working in at least 180 sampled ECEC settings were selected for each target 
population (ISCED Level 02 and settings for children under the age of 3). In consultation with the 
international consortium, national centres prepared individualised survey schedules within the given 
international timeline. The field trial and main survey were carried out according to the technical standards, 
manuals, and guidelines to ensure high response rates and high-quality data. 
Given the positive experience and number of participants who completed the TALIS 2018 and TALIS 
Starting Strong 2018 questionnaires online, this delivery mode is defined as the main mode of 

   113 
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
questionnaire administration in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. Online questionnaire administration offers a 
number of operational benefits, including a significant reduction of paper handling and data capture costs 
for national centres. Online data collection also improves the administration of questionnaires as it is more 
flexible, adaptive, and efficient. For example, filter questions can guide respondents through the 
questionnaire, inconsistencies in responses can be checked in real time, and no manual data entry needs 
to be planned and organised. However, paper questionnaires will be available as needed to ensure all 
selected staff members can access to the survey. 
All questionnaires were made available to participating countries and jurisdictions in English. For the field 
trial and the main survey, questionnaires were adapted and translated at the national centres and 
submitted for international translation verification using the IEA Study Expert. National centres were trained 
in adapting and translating the instruments into their local language(s) in electronic form, and in how to 
deliver the questionnaires using the IEA Study Expert. During main survey data collection, the Data Monitor 
provided national centres with the opportunity to monitor the questionnaire return status and the status of 
questionnaire completion at any time, allowing them to keep track of response rates. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 requires detailed attention to all aspects of survey quality and quality control 
measures. Quality observation measures were implemented in the following areas of activities: 
• 
technical standards, operational manuals, guidelines 
• 
sampling plan implementation 
• 
instrument preparation, including national adaptations, translation and translation verification, 
and layout verification 
• 
survey implementation and data collection 
• 
international and national quality observation monitoring of data collection 
• 
data entry, processing and products 
• 
weighting 
• 
adjudication 
• 
analysis and report production. 
Standards, operational manuals, and guidelines define the rules that national centres are asked to follow 
when preparing and implementing TALIS Starting Strong 2024. Special attention was given to the training 
of national project managers (NPMs) and their staff to enable them to fulfil all required tasks and activities 
to the highest quality possible. 
In international comparative surveys like TALIS Starting Strong 2024, it is of utmost importance to apply 
instruments identical in meaning, wording, and style in all participating countries and jurisdictions and their 
languages. Several quality control steps were developed to ensure comparability of the instruments. First, 
mandatory and optional national adaptations were prepared by the national centres and approved by the 
international research consortium. Second, instruments were translated by national centres following 
international standards and procedures defined in the Survey Operations Procedures (SOP) units, 
technical standards, and manuals. Third, all translated instruments went through a translation verification 
procedure, which flagged any deviation of the translated instruments from the source versions. Intensive 
communication and guidance during the translation/verification process were put in place to guarantee 
high-quality survey instruments. All adaptations and acceptable deviations from the source versions of the 
questionnaires were documented and considered during data processing and adjudication. 
International quality observation monitoring was also a central part of the quality control measures of TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024. An International Quality Observation Programme was implemented, and 
international quality observers (IQOs) from each participating country were trained to conduct the 
programme. In addition, a National Quality Observation Manual, as well as training and guidelines for 
NPMs, were provided to prepare and implement national quality observation measures. 

114    
TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 CONCEPTUAL FRAMEWORK © OECD 2025 
  
Following data collection, NPMs were required to comply with the standards and guidelines described in 
the SOPs, as well as attend a data management training. 
Data submission by the national centres were monitored closely by the international consortium to verify 
the completeness and quality of the data received. 
A fully documented international database containing ECEC setting-related responses, together with the 
survey weights to allow published estimates to be reproduced and original analyses to be conducted, will 
be made available free of charge. Additionally, a technical report documenting the methods and 
procedures used in developing and implementing TALIS Starting Strong 2024 and analysis guidelines will 
be prepared and published.

Notes
 
1 It is important to note that, while there may not always be one-to-one correspondence between the so-labelled cross-
cutting content areas in TALIS Starting Strong 2024 and TALIS 2024, there is significant overlap in the two surveys’ 
priority areas. 
2 While social and emotional learning is not officially named as a “cross-cutting content area” in TALIS Starting Strong 
2024, social and emotional learning is embedded across different content areas. For example, supporting emotional 
development and well-being and supporting pro-social behaviour represent two of the eight sub-domains of process 
quality, as it is operationalised in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. In addition, approaches to comprehensive/continuous 
child supports include (but are not limited to) beliefs about and practices that contribute to children’s holistic 
development, including elements of social and emotional learning, such as self-regulation and pro-social behaviour. 
3 Environmental sustainability education appears to a limited extent in TALIS Starting Strong 2024, with a few 
questions asking about approaches used by ECEC practitioners to develop children’s early environmental awareness.

The OECD Starting Strong Teaching and Learning International Survey (TALIS Starting Strong) is the first international survey that focuses on the 
early childhood education and care (ECEC) workforce. The TALIS Starting Strong 2024 conceptual framework builds on foundations established in 
2018 and links the survey’s areas of focus and policy questions to existing knowledge. 
Key themes in the framework include staff practices with children, staff and leader education, professional development, well-being and job 
satisfaction, as well as terms of employment and intent to stay in the profession. It also addresses themes related to diversity in ECEC settings, 
providing comprehensive child supports, fostering competent settings, and the use of digital technologies to support staff and leaders, all in the 
context of setting resources and leadership and structural features.
